(This agrees with Step 2(b), where $\beta = \frac12$ was exactly the case in which the dilation scaling maps solutions to solutions.)

\paragraph{Step 5: radial $v$.}
We simplify further by guessing $v$ to be radial; that is, $v(y) = w(|y|)$ for some $w : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$. Thereupon \eqref{eq:heat6} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:heatradial}
\alpha w + \frac12 r w' + w'' + \frac{n-1}{r}w' = 0,
\end{equation}
for $r = |y|$, $' = \frac{d}{dr}$.

\emph{Why radial?} The Laplacian is invariant under rotations (Section~\ref{ssec:rotinv}): diffusion has no preferred direction. Also \eqref{eq:heat6} is still a PDE in the $n$ variables $y_1,\dots,y_n$; assuming $v$ radial reduces it to an ODE in the single variable $r$, exactly as we did for Laplace's equation.

\emph{The computation} (for $y \neq 0$). Let $r = |y|$. As in Section~\ref{sssec:radlap}, $\frac{\partial r}{\partial y_i} = \frac{y_i}{r}$, so
$$ v_{y_i} = w'(r)\frac{y_i}{r}, \qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad Dv(y) = w'(r)\frac{y}{r}. $$
Therefore
$$ y\cdot Dv(y) = w'(r)\,\frac{y\cdot y}{r} = w'(r)\frac{|y|^2}{r} = w'(r)\frac{r^2}{r} = r\,w'(r). $$
The radial formula for the Laplacian (Section~\ref{sssec:radlap}) gives
$$ \Delta v(y) = w''(r) + \frac{n-1}{r}w'(r). $$
Substituting $v = w$, $y\cdot Dv = rw'$ and this expression for $\Delta v$ into \eqref{eq:heat6} gives \eqref{eq:heatradial}.

\paragraph{Step 6: the choice $\alpha = \frac n2$ and solving the ODE.}
Now if we set $\alpha = \frac n2$, this simplifies to read
$$ (r^{n-1}w')' + \frac12(r^n w)' = 0. $$

To see this, multiply \eqref{eq:heatradial} by $r^{n-1}$:
$$ r^{n-1}w'' + (n-1)r^{n-2}w' + \alpha r^{n-1} w + \frac12 r^n w' = 0. $$
\begin{itemize}
    \item The first two terms form an exact derivative: by the product rule $(r^{n-1}w')' = r^{n-1}w'' + (n-1)r^{n-2}w'$.
    \item The last two terms: $(r^n w)' = n r^{n-1}w + r^n w'$, so $\frac12(r^nw)' = \frac n2 r^{n-1}w + \frac12 r^n w'$. This equals $\alpha r^{n-1}w + \frac12 r^nw'$ \emph{exactly when} $\alpha = \frac n2$.
\end{itemize}
So $\alpha = \frac n2$ is the choice which makes the whole left side an exact derivative. (There is also a physical reason for this choice; see the remark after the Lemma below.)

Thus
$$ r^{n-1}w' + \frac12 r^n w = a $$
for some constant $a$: a function whose derivative is zero on the interval $(0,\infty)$ is constant. Assuming $\lim_{r\to\infty} w, w' = 0$, we conclude $a = 0$. (More precisely we need $r^{n-1}w'(r) \to 0$ and $r^n w(r) \to 0$ as $r \to \infty$; that is, $w$ and $w'$ decay fast. We are \emph{looking for} a solution, so we are free to impose this; the function we find, a Gaussian, decays faster than any power of $r$ and does satisfy it.) Letting $r \to \infty$ in the identity, the left side tends to $0$, so $a = 0$; whence, dividing by $r^{n-1} > 0$,
$$ w' = -\frac12 rw. $$
But then for some constant $b$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:heatw}
w = be^{-\frac{r^2}{4}}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, multiply by the integrating factor $e^{r^2/4}$ (note $\frac{d}{dr}e^{r^2/4} = \frac r2 e^{r^2/4}$):
$$ \frac{d}{dr}\Big(e^{\frac{r^2}{4}}w\Big) = e^{\frac{r^2}{4}}\Big(w' + \frac r2 w\Big) = 0, $$
so $e^{r^2/4}w(r) = b$ is constant, which is \eqref{eq:heatw}. (This avoids dividing by $w$, which might vanish.)

\paragraph{Step 7: conclusion.}
Combining \eqref{eq:ansatz}, \eqref{eq:heatw} and our choices for $\alpha, \beta$, we conclude that $\frac{b}{t^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}}$ solves the heat equation \eqref{eq:heat}.

Indeed, with $\alpha = \frac n2$, $\beta = \frac12$ we have $y = \frac{x}{\sqrt t}$, $r = |y| = \frac{|x|}{\sqrt t}$, $\frac{r^2}{4} = \frac{|x|^2}{4t}$, and $v(y) = w(|y|) = be^{-|y|^2/4}$, so
$$ u(x,t) = \frac{1}{t^{n/2}}\, v\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt t}\Big) = \frac{b}{t^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}}. $$

\emph{Direct check.} Since the derivation contained guesses, let us verify directly that $u(x,t) = b\,t^{-n/2}e^{-|x|^2/4t}$ solves \eqref{eq:heat} for $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$, $t > 0$. Write $E = e^{-|x|^2/4t}$. Using $\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\big(-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}\big) = \frac{|x|^2}{4t^2}$,
$$ u_t = b\Big(-\frac n2 t^{-\frac n2 - 1}E + t^{-\frac n2}\frac{|x|^2}{4t^2}E\Big) = b\,t^{-\frac n2}E\Big(\frac{|x|^2}{4t^2} - \frac{n}{2t}\Big). $$
Using $\frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\big(-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}\big) = -\frac{x_i}{2t}$,
$$ u_{x_i} = b\,t^{-\frac n2}E\Big(-\frac{x_i}{2t}\Big), \qquad u_{x_ix_i} = b\,t^{-\frac n2}\Big[E\Big(-\frac{x_i}{2t}\Big)^2 + E\Big(-\frac{1}{2t}\Big)\Big] = b\,t^{-\frac n2}E\Big(\frac{x_i^2}{4t^2} - \frac{1}{2t}\Big). $$
Summing over $i$, $\Delta u = b\,t^{-\frac n2}E\big(\frac{|x|^2}{4t^2} - \frac{n}{2t}\big) = u_t$. \checkmark

This computation motivates the following

\begin{definition}[Fundamental solution of the heat equation]\label{def:heatPhi}
The function
$$ \Phi(x,t) := \begin{cases} \dfrac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\, e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}} & (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t > 0) \\[1em] 0 & (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t < 0) \end{cases} $$
is called the \emph{fundamental solution} of the heat equation.
\end{definition}

Notice that $\Phi$ is singular at the point $(0,0)$. We will sometimes write $\Phi(x,t) = \Phi(|x|,t)$ to emphasize that the fundamental solution is radial in the variable $x$. The choice of the normalizing constant $(4\pi)^{-n/2}$ is dictated by the following Lemma.

\emph{About the singularity.} $\Phi$ is not defined at $t = 0$, and it cannot be extended continuously to $(0,0)$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item along $x = 0$: $\Phi(0,t) = (4\pi t)^{-n/2} \to +\infty$ as $t \to 0^+$;
    \item for fixed $x \neq 0$: $\Phi(x,t) \to 0$ as $t \to 0^+$ (put $s = \frac1t \to \infty$; then $\Phi = (4\pi)^{-n/2}s^{n/2}e^{-s|x|^2/4}$, and the exponential decay beats the power growth).
\end{itemize}
So as $t \downarrow 0$ the function $\Phi(\cdot,t)$ becomes very large near $x = 0$ and very small away from $x = 0$. Compare $\Phi(x) = \frac{1}{n(n-2)\alpha(n)}|x|^{2-n}$ for Laplace's equation ($n \ge 3$), which is singular at $x = 0$.

\begin{lemma}[Integral of fundamental solution]\label{lem:heatint}
For each time $t > 0$,
$$ \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x,t)\, dx = 1. $$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We calculate
\begin{align*}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x,t)\, dx &= \frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}}\, dx \\
&= \frac{1}{\pi^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} e^{-|z|^2}\, dz \\
&= \frac{1}{\pi^{n/2}}\prod_{i=1}^n\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-z_i^2}\, dz_i = 1.
\end{align*}

Let us justify each equality.

\emph{First line.} For $t > 0$ this is the definition of $\Phi$; the constant $(4\pi t)^{-n/2}$ does not depend on $x$ and comes out of the integral.

\emph{Second line: change of variables $x = 2\sqrt t\, z$.} Fix $t > 0$. Then $\frac{|x|^2}{4t} = \frac{4t|z|^2}{4t} = |z|^2$. The map $z \mapsto 2\sqrt t\, z$ is linear, multiplying each of the $n$ coordinates by $2\sqrt t$; its Jacobian determinant is $(2\sqrt t)^n = 2^n t^{n/2}$, so $dx = 2^n t^{n/2}\, dz$ (as $z$ runs over $\mathbb{R}^n$, so does $x$). Hence
$$ \frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}}\, dx = \frac{2^n t^{n/2}}{4^{n/2}\pi^{n/2}t^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} e^{-|z|^2}\, dz = \frac{1}{\pi^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} e^{-|z|^2}\, dz, $$
since $4^{n/2} = 2^n$.

\emph{Third line: splitting into one-dimensional integrals.} Since $|z|^2 = z_1^2 + \cdots + z_n^2$,
$$ e^{-|z|^2} = e^{-z_1^2}e^{-z_2^2}\cdots e^{-z_n^2}. $$
The integrand is nonnegative, so by Fubini--Tonelli (Fact~\ref{fact:measure}) we may integrate in $z_1, \dots, z_n$ one at a time, in any order; each factor depends on one variable only and comes out of the integrals in the other variables. This gives the product of the $n$ one-dimensional integrals.

\emph{Last equality: the Gaussian integral.} We show $I := \int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-s^2}\, ds = \sqrt\pi$; then the product is $(\sqrt\pi)^n = \pi^{n/2}$, which cancels the factor $\pi^{-n/2}$. First, $I$ is finite: $e^{-s^2} \le e^{1-2|s|}$ for all $s$ (because $s^2 - 2|s| + 1 = (|s|-1)^2 \geq 0$), and $\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{1-2|s|}\, ds = e < \infty$. Next, by the case $n = 2$ of what we just did (Tonelli),
$$ I^2 = \int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-z_1^2}\, dz_1\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-z_2^2}\, dz_2 = \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} e^{-|z|^2}\, dz. $$
The integrand is radial, $e^{-|z|^2} = g(|z|)$ with $g(r) = e^{-r^2}$. By polar coordinates (Corollary~\ref{cor:polar}(c), with $n = 2$, $n\alpha(n) = 2\pi$),
$$ \int_{B(0,R)} e^{-|z|^2}\, dz = 2\pi\int_0^R e^{-r^2}\, r\, dr = 2\pi\Big[-\frac12 e^{-r^2}\Big]_0^R = \pi\big(1 - e^{-R^2}\big). $$
As $R \to \infty$ the left side increases to $\int_{\mathbb{R}^2} e^{-|z|^2}\, dz$ (Monotone Convergence Theorem, Fact~\ref{fact:measure}, applied to $e^{-|z|^2}\chi_{B(0,R)}$), and the right side tends to $\pi$. So $I^2 = \pi$, and since $I > 0$, $I = \sqrt\pi$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why $\alpha = \frac n2$ and why the constant $(4\pi)^{-n/2}$]
(i) For any $\alpha$ and $\beta = \frac12$, the change of variables $x = \sqrt t\, y$ (so $dx = t^{n/2}dy$) gives
$$ \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\frac{1}{t^\alpha}\, v\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt t}\Big)\, dx = t^{\frac n2 - \alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} v(y)\, dy. $$
If $\int v \ne 0$, this is independent of $t$ exactly when $\alpha = \frac n2$. In the physical interpretation, $\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} u(x,t)\,dx$ is the total amount of heat at time $t$, and with no source it should not change in time. So the choice $\alpha = \frac n2$, which made the ODE exactly integrable in Step 6, is also the one which conserves the total heat.

(ii) The constant $b$ in Step 7 is arbitrary (the equation is linear). We choose $b = (4\pi)^{-n/2}$ so that the total heat is exactly $1$ at every time (Lemma~\ref{lem:heatint}). Together with the behaviour as $t \to 0^+$ described after the Definition, this says: $\Phi(\cdot,t)$ describes a unit amount of heat which at time $0$ is concentrated at the origin and then spreads out.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Problem.} Assume $n = 1$ and $u(x,t) = v\big(\frac{x^2}{t}\big)$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)] Show $u_t = u_{xx}$ if and only if
    $$ 4zv''(z) + (2 + z)v'(z) = 0 \qquad (z > 0). $$
    \item[(b)] Show that the general solution of the ODE in (a) is
    $$ v(z) = c\int_0^z e^{-s/4}s^{-1/2}\, ds + d. $$
    \item[(c)] Differentiate $v\big(\frac{x^2}{t}\big)$ with respect to $x$ and select the constant $c$ properly, so as to obtain the fundamental solution $\Phi$ for $n = 1$. Explain why this procedure produces the fundamental solution.
\end{enumerate}

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Initial-value problem}\label{sssec:ivp}

We now employ $\Phi$ to fashion a solution to the \emph{initial-value} (or \emph{Cauchy}) \emph{problem}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ivp}
\begin{cases} u_t - \Delta u = 0 & \text{in } \mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty) \\ u = g & \text{on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \{t = 0\}. \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Here $g : \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ is the given \emph{initial temperature}. We look for a function $u$ which solves the heat equation for all positive times and equals $g$ at time $0$.

Let us note the function $(x,t) \mapsto \Phi(x,t)$ solves the heat equation away from the singularity at $(0,0)$, and thus so does $(x,t) \mapsto \Phi(x - y, t)$ for each fixed $y \in \mathbb{R}^n$. Consequently the convolution
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ivpsol}
\begin{aligned}
u(x,t) &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x - y, t)\, g(y)\, dy \\
&= \frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4t}}\, g(y)\, dy \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t > 0)
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
should also be a solution.

\emph{Why is this a reasonable guess?}
\begin{itemize}
    \item For $t > 0$, $\Phi$ solves the heat equation (Step 7 of the derivation, with $b = (4\pi)^{-n/2}$). For $t < 0$, $\Phi \equiv 0$, which trivially solves it. So $\Phi$ solves the heat equation at every point except possibly where it is not smooth, the point $(0,0)$.
    \item For fixed $y$, the function $(x,t) \mapsto \Phi(x - y, t)$ is $\Phi$ shifted in space so that its singularity sits at $(y,0)$. Shifting does not change derivatives: by the chain rule, with inner map $x \mapsto x - y$ whose derivative is the identity, $\frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}[\Phi(x-y,t)] = \Phi_{x_i}(x - y,t)$, $\frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_i^2}[\Phi(x-y,t)] = \Phi_{x_ix_i}(x - y,t)$ and $\frac{\partial}{\partial t}[\Phi(x-y,t)] = \Phi_t(x-y,t)$. Hence it is again a solution for $t > 0$.
    \item The heat equation is linear: sums and constant multiples of solutions are solutions. The integral $\int \Phi(x-y,t)g(y)\,dy$ is a ``continuous sum'' of the solutions $\Phi(x - y,t)$ with weights $g(y)$, so it should again be a solution.
    \item As $t \to 0^+$, $\Phi(\cdot - y,t)$ concentrates its unit mass at the point $y$ (see the remarks after the Definition of $\Phi$). So $u(x,t)$ is an \emph{average} of the values $g(y)$ with weights $\Phi(x-y,t)$, and for small $t$ almost all the weight sits at $y$ near $x$. So we expect $u(x,t) \to g(x)$ as $t \to 0^+$.
\end{itemize}

\emph{$u$ is well defined.} Fix $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $t > 0$. Since $\Phi > 0$ and $|g| \leq \|g\|_{L^\infty}$ everywhere (Definition~\ref{def:Lp}),
$$ |\Phi(x - y,t)\, g(y)| \leq \|g\|_{L^\infty}\, \Phi(x - y,t), \qquad\text{and}\qquad \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x - y,t)\, dy = 1. $$
The last identity follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:heatint} by the change of variables $z = x - y$ (a translation followed by the reflection $y \mapsto -y$; its Jacobian determinant has absolute value $1$, and $z$ runs over all of $\mathbb{R}^n$ as $y$ does):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mass1}
\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x - y,t)\, dy = \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(z,t)\, dz = 1 \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t > 0).
\end{equation}
So the integral in \eqref{eq:ivpsol} converges absolutely and $|u(x,t)| \leq \|g\|_{L^\infty}$.

\bigskip
\textbf{Theorem 1 (Solution of initial-value problem).} \textit{Assume $g \in C(\mathbb{R}^n) \cap L^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$, and define $u$ by \eqref{eq:ivpsol}. Then}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(i)] $u \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty))$,
    \item[(ii)] $u_t(x,t) - \Delta u(x,t) = 0 \quad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t > 0)$,
\end{enumerate}
\textit{and}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(iii)] $\displaystyle\lim_{\substack{(x,t)\to(x^0,0)\\ x\in\mathbb{R}^n,\ t>0}} u(x,t) = g(x^0)$ \textit{for each point $x^0 \in \mathbb{R}^n$.}
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Reading the statement.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item The hypothesis $g \in C(\mathbb{R}^n)\cap L^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$ just means $g$ is continuous and bounded (Definition~\ref{def:Lp}). Boundedness makes the integral \eqref{eq:ivpsol} converge; continuity is used in (iii).
    \item (i) says $u$ is infinitely differentiable for $t > 0$, even though $g$ is only continuous. The heat equation \emph{smooths} the initial data instantly.
    \item (ii) says $u$ solves the PDE in \eqref{eq:ivp}.
    \item The formula \eqref{eq:ivpsol} makes no sense at $t = 0$ (there is a factor $t^{-n/2}$). So the condition ``$u = g$ on $t = 0$'' in \eqref{eq:ivp} is understood in the sense of (iii): as $(x,t)$ approaches a point $(x^0,0)$ of the initial plane, \emph{from inside the region $t > 0$ and from any direction}, $u(x,t)$ tends to $g(x^0)$. Equivalently: if we \emph{define} $u(x,0) := g(x)$, then $u$ is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$.
    \item In (iii) both $x$ and $t$ move. It is \emph{not} enough to fix $x = x^0$ and let $t \to 0^+$; this is what makes (iii) the hard part.
\end{itemize}

%-----------------------------------------
\paragraph{Proof of (i).} Since the function $\frac{1}{t^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}}$ is infinitely differentiable, with uniformly bounded derivatives of all orders, on $\mathbb{R}^n \times [\delta,\infty)$ for each $\delta > 0$, we see that $u \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty))$.

The idea: we want to differentiate \eqref{eq:ivpsol} under the integral sign as often as we like. Then every derivative of $u$ is $\int (\text{derivative of }\Phi)(x-y,t)\, g(y)\, dy$, and these are continuous. The tool is Lemma~\ref{lem:diffintRn}, with $z = (x,t)$, $W = \mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)$ (an open subset of $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$), and
$$ F(x,t,y) = \Phi(x - y,t)\, g(y). $$
$F$ is continuous, and smooth in $(x,t)$ for $t > 0$. What we must check is the \emph{domination}: near each point $(x_0,t_0)$, every derivative $D^\alpha_{(x,t)}F$ is bounded by one integrable function $G(y)$. Since we integrate over all of $\mathbb{R}^n$, which has infinite volume, it is not enough that the derivatives are bounded; we also need decay in $y$. Both come from the Gaussian factor. We proceed in three steps.

\emph{Step A: the form of the derivatives.} Write $K(x,t) := t^{-n/2}e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}}$, so $\Phi = (4\pi)^{-n/2}K$ for $t > 0$, and write $E := e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{4t}}$. We claim: \emph{every partial derivative of $K$ of any order, in the variables $x_1,\dots,x_n,t$, is a finite sum of terms of the form}
$$ c\; t^{-m}\, x^\gamma\, E, \qquad c \in \mathbb{R},\ m \geq 0,\ \gamma \text{ a multi-index},\ x^\gamma = x_1^{\gamma_1}\cdots x_n^{\gamma_n}. $$
$K$ itself has this form ($c = 1$, $m = \frac n2$, $\gamma = 0$). It suffices to show that the derivative of one such term, with respect to $t$ or any $x_i$, is again a sum of such terms; then the claim follows by induction on the order. Using $E_t = \frac{|x|^2}{4t^2}E$ and $E_{x_i} = -\frac{x_i}{2t}E$:
\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\big(t^{-m}x^\gamma E\big) &= -m\,t^{-m-1}x^\gamma E + \frac14\, t^{-m-2}\, x^\gamma |x|^2 E, \\
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\big(t^{-m}x^\gamma E\big) &= \gamma_i\, t^{-m}x^{\gamma - e_i}E - \frac12\, t^{-m-1}x^{\gamma+e_i}E,
\end{align*}
(the first term in the second line is absent if $\gamma_i = 0$), and $x^\gamma|x|^2 = \sum_j x^{\gamma + 2e_j}$ is a sum of monomials. So all terms are again of the required form. In particular $K$ is $C^\infty$ on $\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)$: it is built from smooth functions by products and compositions.

\emph{Step B: bounds for $t$ in a compact interval.} Let $0 < \delta \leq t \leq T$. Since each $|x_i| \leq |x|$, we have $|x^\gamma| \leq |x|^{|\gamma|}$. Also $t^{-m} \leq \delta^{-m}$ and $e^{-|x|^2/4t} \leq e^{-|x|^2/4T}$ (because $\frac{1}{4t} \geq \frac{1}{4T}$). So each term satisfies
$$ |c\, t^{-m}x^\gamma E| \leq |c|\,\delta^{-m}\,|x|^{|\gamma|}e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{8T}}\cdot e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{8T}} \leq |c|\,\delta^{-m}M\, e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{8T}}, $$
where we split the exponential into two halves and used that $s \mapsto s^{k}e^{-s^2/8T}$ is continuous on $[0,\infty)$ and tends to $0$ as $s \to \infty$, hence is bounded by some $M$ (with $k = |\gamma|$). Adding the finitely many terms: \emph{for each derivative $D^\alpha K$ there is a constant $C$ (depending on $\alpha, \delta, T, n$) with}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:DKbound}
|D^\alpha K(x,t)| \leq C\, e^{-\frac{|x|^2}{8T}} \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ \delta \leq t \leq T).
\end{equation}
In particular, all derivatives of $K$ are bounded on $\mathbb{R}^n \times [\delta,T]$. (Bounded even on $\mathbb{R}^n \times [\delta,\infty)$, since $t^{-m}|x|^ke^{-|x|^2/4t} = t^{k/2-m}\,(|x|/\sqrt t)^ke^{-|x|^2/4t}$; we will not need this.)

\emph{Step C: domination near a point.} Fix $(x_0,t_0)$ with $t_0 > 0$. Take $\rho = \min\{1, \frac{t_0}{2}\}$, $\delta = \frac{t_0}{2}$, $T = 2t_0$. If $(x,t) \in B((x_0,t_0),\rho)$, then $|x - x_0| < 1$ and $\delta < t < T$. By the chain rule (second item of ``Why is this a reasonable guess?''), $D^\alpha_{(x,t)}[\Phi(x-y,t)] = (D^\alpha\Phi)(x - y,t)$, so by \eqref{eq:DKbound},
$$ |D^\alpha_{(x,t)}F(x,t,y)| = |(D^\alpha\Phi)(x-y,t)|\,|g(y)| \leq C\,\|g\|_{L^\infty}\, e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{8T}}. $$
The right side still depends on $x$; we need a bound by a function of $y$ alone. From $|y - x_0| \leq |y - x| + |x - x_0|$ and $(a + b)^2 \leq 2a^2 + 2b^2$,
$$ |y - x_0|^2 \leq 2|x - y|^2 + 2|x - x_0|^2 \leq 2|x-y|^2 + 2, \qquad\text{so}\qquad |x - y|^2 \geq \tfrac12|y - x_0|^2 - 1. $$
Hence $e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{8T}} \leq e^{\frac{1}{8T}}\,e^{-\frac{|y-x_0|^2}{16T}}$, and
$$ |D^\alpha_{(x,t)}F(x,t,y)| \leq G(y) := C'\, e^{-\frac{|y-x_0|^2}{16T}} \qquad ((x,t) \in B((x_0,t_0),\rho),\ y \in \mathbb{R}^n), $$
with $C' = C\|g\|_{L^\infty}e^{1/8T}$. (For finitely many $\alpha$ at a time we take the largest of the constants.) $G$ is integrable: it is a constant multiple of a Gaussian, and by the change of variables $y = x_0 + 4\sqrt T z$ and Lemma~\ref{lem:heatint}'s proof, $\int e^{-|y - x_0|^2/16T}dy = (16\pi T)^{n/2} < \infty$.

\emph{Conclusion.} The hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:diffintRn} hold for all orders, so $u \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty))$, and every derivative may be taken inside the integral:
$$ D^\alpha u(x,t) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}(D^\alpha\Phi)(x-y,t)\, g(y)\, dy. $$

%-----------------------------------------
\paragraph{Proof of (ii).} Furthermore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:heat10}
\begin{aligned}
u_t(x,t) - \Delta u(x,t) &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\big[(\Phi_t - \Delta_x\Phi)(x - y,t)\big]\, g(y)\, dy \\
&= 0 \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t > 0),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
since $\Phi$ itself solves the heat equation.

In detail: by the formula for $D^\alpha u$ just proved, with $\alpha$ corresponding to $\partial_t$ and to $\partial_{x_i}^2$,
$$ u_t(x,t) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi_t(x-y,t)\,g(y)\,dy, \qquad u_{x_ix_i}(x,t) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi_{x_ix_i}(x-y,t)\,g(y)\,dy. $$
Sum the second over $i = 1,\dots,n$ and subtract from the first; the integrals combine by linearity. For $t > 0$ the point $(x - y,t)$ has positive time coordinate, so it is not the singular point, and $(\Phi_t - \Delta_x\Phi)(x-y,t) = 0$ by Step~7 of the derivation. The integrand is identically zero, so the integral is zero. $\square$

%-----------------------------------------
\paragraph{Proof of (iii): the idea.}
Two $\varepsilon$--$\delta$ statements are involved.

\emph{What we know (continuity of $g$ at $x^0$).} For every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $\delta > 0$ such that $|g(y) - g(x^0)| < \varepsilon$ whenever $|y - x^0| < \delta$.

\emph{What we want (the limit in (iii)).} For every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $\eta > 0$ such that
$$ |u(x,t) - g(x^0)| < \varepsilon \qquad \text{whenever } |x - x^0| < \eta \text{ and } 0 < t < \eta. $$
(Requiring $|x - x^0| < \eta$ and $0 < t < \eta$ is the same as requiring that $(x,t)$ is close to $(x^0,0)$ in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$, up to changing $\eta$: a ball of radius $\eta$ around $(x^0,0)$ lies inside this box, and the box lies inside the ball of radius $\sqrt2\,\eta$.)

\emph{How to get the second from the first.} Three observations.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{Write $g(x^0)$ as an average.} By \eqref{eq:mass1}, $g(x^0) = g(x^0)\int\Phi(x - y,t)\,dy = \int\Phi(x - y,t)\,g(x^0)\,dy$: a constant is its own weighted average, because the weights have total mass $1$. Therefore
    $$ u(x,t) - g(x^0) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x-y,t)\,\big[g(y) - g(x^0)\big]\, dy. $$
    So we must show: the weighted average of $g(y) - g(x^0)$ is small.
    \item \emph{Near $x^0$, the integrand is small because $g$ is continuous.} On the ball $|y - x^0| < \delta$ we have $|g(y) - g(x^0)| < \varepsilon$. The weights are nonnegative with total mass $1$, so this part contributes at most $\varepsilon$. This works for every $t > 0$ and every $x$.
    \item \emph{Far from $x^0$, the weight is small because $t$ is small.} On $|y - x^0| \geq \delta$ we only know $|g(y) - g(x^0)| \leq 2\|g\|_{L^\infty}$. But if $x$ is close to $x^0$ (say $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$), then such $y$ are far from $x$ too (at distance $\ge \frac{\delta}{2}$ or so), and as $t \to 0^+$ the heat kernel $\Phi(x-y,t)$ puts almost no mass at points far from $x$. So this part tends to $0$ as $t \to 0^+$, and the bound we get will not depend on $x$.
\end{enumerate}
Putting these together: the near part is $< \varepsilon$, the far part is $< \varepsilon$ for small $t$, so $|u(x,t) - g(x^0)| < 2\varepsilon$ when $|x - x^0| < \frac{\delta}{2}$ and $t$ is small. Since $\varepsilon > 0$ is arbitrary, ``$< 2\varepsilon$'' is as good as ``$< \varepsilon$'' (run the argument with $\frac\varepsilon2$ in place of $\varepsilon$).

The point of the splitting: continuity of $g$ controls $g(y) - g(x^0)$ only for $y$ near $x^0$, and the concentration of $\Phi$ controls the weights only for $y$ far from $x$. We split the integral so that each part is handled by the fact that applies to it.

%-----------------------------------------
\paragraph{Proof of (iii): the details.}

\emph{Step 1: choice of $\delta$.} Fix $x^0 \in \mathbb{R}^n$, $\varepsilon > 0$. Choose $\delta > 0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gcont}
|g(y) - g(x^0)| < \varepsilon \quad \text{if } |y - x^0| < \delta,\ y \in \mathbb{R}^n.
\end{equation}
This is possible because $g$ is continuous at $x^0$. Note that $\delta$ depends only on $x^0$, $\varepsilon$ and $g$; it has nothing to do with $x$ or $t$.

\emph{Step 2: splitting the integral.} Then if $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$, we have, according to the lemma,
\begin{align*}
|u(x,t) - g(x^0)| &= \Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x - y,t)\,[g(y) - g(x^0)]\, dy\Big| \\
&\leq \int_{B(x^0,\delta)}\Phi(x-y,t)\,|g(y) - g(x^0)|\, dy \\
&\qquad + \int_{\mathbb{R}^n - B(x^0,\delta)}\Phi(x-y,t)\,|g(y) - g(x^0)|\, dy \\
&=: I + J.
\end{align*}
Explanation of each line:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The first equality is observation 1 of the idea: $\int\Phi(x-y,t)\,dy = 1$ by \eqref{eq:mass1} (this is where ``the lemma'', Lemma~\ref{lem:heatint}, is used), so $g(x^0) = \int\Phi(x-y,t)\,g(x^0)\,dy$, and we subtract the two integrals.
    \item The inequality: the absolute value of an integral is at most the integral of the absolute value, and $|\Phi(x - y,t)\,[g(y) - g(x^0)]| = \Phi(x-y,t)\,|g(y) - g(x^0)|$ because $\Phi > 0$. Then we split $\mathbb{R}^n$ into the ball $B(x^0,\delta)$ and its complement $\mathbb{R}^n - B(x^0,\delta)$. Both integrals are finite: the integrand is at most $2\|g\|_{L^\infty}\Phi(x-y,t)$.
\end{itemize}
Note that the ball is centred at $x^0$, not at $x$: it is the ball on which \eqref{eq:gcont} holds.

\emph{Step 3: estimate of $I$ (the near part).} Now
$$ I \leq \varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}\Phi(x - y,t)\, dy = \varepsilon, $$
owing to \eqref{eq:gcont} and the lemma. Indeed, for $y \in B(x^0,\delta)$, \eqref{eq:gcont} gives $|g(y) - g(x^0)| < \varepsilon$, and $\Phi \geq 0$, so the integrand is at most $\varepsilon\,\Phi(x - y,t)$. Then we enlarge the domain of integration from $B(x^0,\delta)$ to $\mathbb{R}^n$, which can only increase the integral since $\Phi \ge 0$, and use \eqref{eq:mass1}. This bound holds for \emph{all} $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and \emph{all} $t > 0$.

\emph{Step 4: a geometric inequality.} Furthermore, if $|x - x^0| \leq \frac\delta2$ and $|y - x^0| \geq \delta$, then
$$ |y - x^0| \leq |y - x| + \frac\delta2 \leq |y - x| + \frac12|y - x^0|. $$
Thus $|y - x| \geq \frac12|y - x^0|$.

Explanation: the first inequality is the triangle inequality $|y - x^0| \leq |y - x| + |x - x^0|$ together with $|x - x^0| \leq \frac\delta2$. The second uses $\delta \leq |y - x^0|$, so $\frac\delta2 \leq \frac12|y - x^0|$. Subtracting $\frac12|y - x^0|$ from both sides of $|y - x^0| \leq |y - x| + \frac12|y-x^0|$ gives $\frac12|y - x^0| \leq |y - x|$.

In words: $x$ is within $\frac\delta2$ of $x^0$, and $y$ is at least $\delta$ away from $x^0$, so $y$ is at least half as far from $x$ as it is from $x^0$. This lets us replace the distance $|x - y|$, which depends on $x$, by $|y - x^0|$, which does not.

\emph{Step 5: estimate of $J$ (the far part).} Consequently
\begin{align*}
J &\leq 2\|g\|_{L^\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n - B(x^0,\delta)}\Phi(x - y,t)\, dy \\
&\leq \frac{C}{t^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n - B(x^0,\delta)} e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4t}}\, dy \\
&\leq \frac{C}{t^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n - B(x^0,\delta)} e^{-\frac{|y-x^0|^2}{16t}}\, dy \\
&= \frac{C}{t^{n/2}}\int_\delta^\infty e^{-\frac{r^2}{16t}}r^{n-1}\, dr \to 0 \quad \text{as } t \to 0^+.
\end{align*}
Here $C$ denotes a constant depending only on $n$ and $\|g\|_{L^\infty}$, which may change from line to line. Line by line:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{First line.} $|g(y) - g(x^0)| \leq |g(y)| + |g(x^0)| \leq 2\|g\|_{L^\infty}$ (for continuous $g$ the bound holds at every point, Definition~\ref{def:Lp}).
    \item \emph{Second line.} Insert the formula $\Phi(x-y,t) = (4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-|x-y|^2/4t}$ (here $t > 0$); $C = 2\|g\|_{L^\infty}(4\pi)^{-n/2}$.
    \item \emph{Third line.} Let $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$. For $y$ in the domain of integration, $|y - x^0| \geq \delta$, so by Step 4, $|x - y| \geq \frac12|y - x^0|$, i.e.\ $|x - y|^2 \geq \frac14|y - x^0|^2$. Dividing by $-4t < 0$ reverses the inequality: $-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4t} \leq -\frac{|y-x^0|^2}{16t}$. Since $s \mapsto e^s$ is increasing, $e^{-|x-y|^2/4t} \leq e^{-|y-x^0|^2/16t}$. \emph{From this line on, $x$ has disappeared}: the bound is the same for all $x$ with $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$.
    \item \emph{Fourth line: polar coordinates around $x^0$.} The integrand is radial about $x^0$: $e^{-|y-x^0|^2/16t} = h(|y - x^0|)$ with $h(r) = e^{-r^2/16t}$. By Corollary~\ref{cor:polar}(c), for $R > \delta$,
    $$ \int_{\delta \le |y - x^0| < R} h(|y - x^0|)\, dy = \int_{B(x^0,R)} - \int_{B(x^0,\delta)} = n\alpha(n)\int_\delta^R e^{-\frac{r^2}{16t}}r^{n-1}\, dr. $$
    (The sphere $|y - x^0| = \delta$ has volume zero, so it does not matter whether it is included.) Letting $R \to \infty$ (Monotone Convergence, Fact~\ref{fact:measure}; the integrand is $\geq 0$) gives the fourth line, with $n\alpha(n)$ absorbed into $C$.
    \item \emph{The limit.} Substitute $r = 4\sqrt t\, s$, so $dr = 4\sqrt t\, ds$, $\frac{r^2}{16t} = s^2$, $r^{n-1} = 4^{n-1}t^{\frac{n-1}{2}}s^{n-1}$, and $r = \delta$ corresponds to $s = \frac{\delta}{4\sqrt t}$:
    $$ \frac{1}{t^{n/2}}\int_\delta^\infty e^{-\frac{r^2}{16t}}r^{n-1}\, dr = \frac{4^n\,t^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\,t^{\frac12}}{t^{n/2}}\int_{\frac{\delta}{4\sqrt t}}^\infty e^{-s^2}s^{n-1}\, ds = 4^n\int_{\frac{\delta}{4\sqrt t}}^\infty e^{-s^2}s^{n-1}\, ds. $$
    \emph{All powers of $t$ cancel}; $t$ remains only in the lower limit. The integral $\int_0^\infty e^{-s^2}s^{n-1}\,ds$ is finite: $s^{n+1}e^{-s^2}$ is bounded on $[0,\infty)$ (continuous, tends to $0$), say by $M$, so $e^{-s^2}s^{n-1} \leq \frac{M}{s^2}$ for $s \geq 1$, which is integrable on $[1,\infty)$, while $[0,1]$ is a bounded interval with a continuous integrand. As $t \to 0^+$ the lower limit $\frac{\delta}{4\sqrt t} \to +\infty$, and the tail $\int_A^\infty$ of a convergent integral tends to $0$ as $A \to \infty$. Hence the last expression tends to $0$.
\end{itemize}
Interpretation: $\int_{|y-x^0|\ge\delta}\Phi(x-y,t)\,dy$ is the amount of the unit mass of $\Phi(x - \cdot,t)$ lying outside $B(x^0,\delta)$; it tends to $0$ as $t \to 0^+$, uniformly for $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$. All the heat stays concentrated near $x$.

\emph{Step 6: conclusion.} Hence if $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$ and $t > 0$ is small enough, $|u(x,t) - g(x^0)| < 2\varepsilon$.

Precisely: by Step 5 there is $t_1 > 0$ (depending on $\delta$, $n$, $\|g\|_{L^\infty}$, hence on $\varepsilon$, but \emph{not} on $x$) such that $J < \varepsilon$ whenever $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$ and $0 < t < t_1$. Put $\eta := \min\{\frac\delta2, t_1\}$. If $|x - x^0| < \eta$ and $0 < t < \eta$, then by Steps 2, 3 and 5
$$ |u(x,t) - g(x^0)| \leq I + J < \varepsilon + \varepsilon = 2\varepsilon. $$
Since $\varepsilon > 0$ was arbitrary, this is the $\varepsilon$--$\eta$ statement of the limit (iii) (with $2\varepsilon$ in place of $\varepsilon$, which does not matter). $\square$

\emph{Why it mattered that the bound for $J$ does not depend on $x$.} We want a limit as $(x,t) \to (x^0,0)$ with $x$ and $t$ moving together. If the bound on $J$ were only good for each fixed $x$, the time $t_1$ might shrink as $x$ moves, and we could not find one $\eta$ working for all $x$ near $x^0$. Step 4 removed $x$ from the estimate, which gave a single $t_1$ for all $|x - x^0| < \frac\delta2$.

%-----------------------------------------
\paragraph{Remarks.}

(i) In view of Theorem 1 we sometimes write
$$ \begin{cases} \Phi_t - \Delta\Phi = 0 & \text{in } \mathbb{R}^n\times(0,\infty) \\ \Phi = \delta_0 & \text{on } \mathbb{R}^n\times\{t = 0\}, \end{cases} $$
$\delta_0$ denoting the Dirac measure on $\mathbb{R}^n$ giving unit mass to the point $0$.

\emph{Explanation.} Compare Section~\ref{ssec:dirac}, where $-\Delta\Phi = \delta_0$ for Laplace's equation was explained. Here: $\Phi$ solves the heat equation for $t > 0$; each $\Phi(\cdot,t)$ has total mass $1$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:heatint}); and as $t \to 0^+$ this mass concentrates at $x = 0$ (Step 5 with $x = x^0 = 0$ shows $\int_{|y| \geq \delta}\Phi(y,t)\,dy \to 0$ for every $\delta > 0$). No function has all its unit mass at one point; the ``initial value'' of $\Phi$ is the Dirac measure $\delta_0$. Formally, \eqref{eq:ivpsol} with $g = \delta_0$ gives $u(x,t) = \int\Phi(x - y,t)\,\delta_0(y)\,dy = \Phi(x,t)$. This is the meaning of the name \emph{fundamental solution}: $\Phi$ is the solution whose initial temperature is a unit amount of heat placed at the origin.

(ii) Notice that if $g$ is bounded, continuous, $g \geq 0$, $g \not\equiv 0$, then
$$ u(x,t) = \frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4t}}\, g(y)\, dy $$
is in fact positive for \emph{all} points $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and times $t > 0$.

\emph{Proof.} Fix $x$ and $t > 0$. Since $g \not\equiv 0$ and $g \geq 0$, there is $y_0$ with $g(y_0) > 0$, and by continuity $g > \frac12 g(y_0)$ on some ball $B(y_0,\rho)$. The exponential $e^{-|x-y|^2/4t}$ is \emph{strictly} positive for every $y$, however far $y$ is from $x$, and on the compact ball $B(y_0,\rho)$ it has a positive minimum $m$. The integrand is $\geq 0$ everywhere, so
$$ u(x,t) \geq \frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{B(y_0,\rho)} e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4t}}\, g(y)\, dy \geq \frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\cdot m\cdot\frac{g(y_0)}{2}\cdot\alpha(n)\rho^n > 0. $$
The point $x$ may be very far from where $g$ is positive, and $t$ may be very small; still $u(x,t) > 0$, because the Gaussian kernel is never zero. $\square$
