\newpage
\section{Wave Equation}\label{sec:wave}

\subsection{Definitions}\label{ssec:wavedef}

In this section we investigate the \textbf{wave equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wave}
u_{tt} - \Delta u = 0
\end{equation}
and the \textbf{nonhomogeneous wave equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wavef}
u_{tt} - \Delta u = f,
\end{equation}
subject to appropriate initial and boundary conditions. Here $t > 0$ and $x \in U$, where $U \subset \mathbb{R}^n$ is open. The unknown is $u : \bar U \times [0,\infty) \to \mathbb{R}$, $u = u(x,t)$, and the Laplacian $\Delta$ is taken with respect to the spatial variables $x = (x_1,\dots,x_n)$. In \eqref{eq:wavef} the function $f : U \times [0,\infty) \to \mathbb{R}$ is given. A common abbreviation is to write
$$ \Box u = u_{tt} - \Delta u. $$

Written out,
$$ u_{tt} - (u_{x_1x_1} + u_{x_2x_2} + \cdots + u_{x_nx_n}) = 0. $$
Compare with the heat equation $u_t - \Delta u = 0$: the only change is that the first time derivative is replaced by the \emph{second} time derivative. The equation is now of second order in $t$ as well as in $x$.

We shall discover that solutions of the wave equation behave quite differently than solutions of Laplace's equation or the heat equation. For example, these solutions are generally not $C^\infty$, exhibit finite speed of propagation, etc.

\paragraph{Initial conditions.} Since \eqref{eq:wave} contains the second derivative $u_{tt}$, it is appropriate to specify \emph{two} initial conditions at time $t = 0$: the values of $u$ and of $u_t$. This is as for a second-order ODE $y'' = F(y)$, where one prescribes $y(0)$ and $y'(0)$. (For the heat equation, which is first order in $t$, we prescribed only $u$ at $t = 0$.)

\paragraph{The case $n = 1$.} For one space variable $x \in \mathbb{R}$, $\Delta u = u_{xx}$ and the wave equation reads
$$ u_{tt} - u_{xx} = 0. $$
This is the case we solve first.

To solve the case $n = 1$ we shall need the transport equation, so we study it first.

\subsection{Transport Equation}\label{ssec:transport}

Probably the simplest partial differential equation of all is the \emph{transport equation} with constant coefficients. This is the PDE
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport}
u_t + b\cdot Du = 0 \qquad \text{in } \mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty),
\end{equation}
where $b$ is a fixed vector in $\mathbb{R}^n$, $b = (b_1,\dots,b_n)$, and $u : \mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty) \to \mathbb{R}$ is the unknown, $u = u(x,t)$. Here $x = (x_1,\dots,x_n) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ denotes a typical point in space, and $t \geq 0$ denotes a typical time. We write $Du = D_xu = (u_{x_1},\dots,u_{x_n})$ for the gradient of $u$ with respect to the spatial variables $x$.

Written out, \eqref{eq:transport} is
$$ u_t + b_1u_{x_1} + b_2u_{x_2} + \cdots + b_nu_{x_n} = 0. $$
It is a linear first-order equation with constant coefficients.

\subsubsection{The key observation: a directional derivative vanishes}

Which functions $u$ solve \eqref{eq:transport}? To answer, let us suppose for the moment we are given some smooth solution $u$ and try to compute it. To do so, we first must recognize that the partial differential equation \eqref{eq:transport} asserts that a particular directional derivative of $u$ vanishes.

Indeed, regard $u$ as a function of the $n+1$ variables $(x,t) = (x_1,\dots,x_n,t)$. Its full gradient in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ is
$$ D_{(x,t)}u = (u_{x_1},\dots,u_{x_n},u_t), $$
and the derivative of $u$ in the direction of the vector $(b,1) = (b_1,\dots,b_n,1) \in \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ is
$$ D_{(x,t)}u\cdot(b,1) = b_1u_{x_1} + \cdots + b_nu_{x_n} + u_t = u_t + b\cdot Du. $$
So \eqref{eq:transport} says exactly: \emph{the derivative of $u$ in the direction $(b,1)$ is zero at every point}. A function whose derivative in a fixed direction vanishes does not change as we move in that direction; so $u$ should be constant along every straight line with direction $(b,1)$. We now make this precise.

We exploit this insight by fixing any point $(x,t) \in \mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)$ and defining
$$ z(s) := u(x + sb, t + s) \qquad (s \in \mathbb{R}). $$
As $s$ varies, the point $(x + sb, t + s) = (x,t) + s(b,1)$ runs along the straight line through $(x,t)$ with direction $(b,1)$, and $z(s)$ records the value of $u$ there. (Since $u$ is defined only for times $\ge 0$, and \eqref{eq:transport} holds only for times $> 0$, $z(s)$ is defined for $t + s \geq 0$, i.e.\ $s \geq -t$, and the equation can be used for $s > -t$; see Lemma~\ref{lem:transconst}.) We then calculate
$$ \dot z(s) = Du(x + sb, t + s)\cdot b + u_t(x + sb, t + s) = 0 \qquad \Big(\dot{} = \frac{d}{ds}\Big), $$
the second equality holding owing to \eqref{eq:transport}. Thus $z(\cdot)$ is a constant function of $s$, and consequently for each point $(x,t)$, $u$ is constant on the line through $(x,t)$ with the direction $(b,1) \in \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$. Hence if we know the value of $u$ at any point on each such line, we know its value everywhere in $\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)$.

Here is the precise statement.

\begin{lemma}[$u$ is constant along the lines]\label{lem:transconst}
Let $u \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty))$ solve \eqref{eq:transport}, and fix $(x,t)$ with $t > 0$. Then $z(s) = u(x + sb, t + s)$ is constant on the interval $(-t,\infty)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{$z$ is defined on $(-t,\infty)$:} for $s > -t$, the point $\gamma(s) := (x + sb, t + s)$ has time coordinate $t + s > 0$, so it lies in $\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)$ where $u$ is defined.

\emph{The derivative of $z$.} The map $\gamma : (-t,\infty) \to \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$, $\gamma(s) = (x_1 + sb_1, \dots, x_n + sb_n, t + s)$ is differentiable, with
$$ \gamma'(s) = (b_1,\dots,b_n,1) = (b,1). $$
Since $u \in C^1$, $u$ is differentiable, and the chain rule gives
$$ \dot z(s) = \frac{d}{ds}u(\gamma(s)) = \sum_{i=1}^n u_{x_i}(\gamma(s))\,b_i + u_t(\gamma(s))\cdot 1 = \big(u_t + b\cdot Du\big)(\gamma(s)). $$
The point $\gamma(s)$ lies in $\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)$, where \eqref{eq:transport} holds; hence $\dot z(s) = 0$ for every $s \in (-t,\infty)$.

\emph{Conclusion.} A differentiable function with zero derivative on an interval is constant there: for $s_1 < s_2$ in $(-t,\infty)$, the mean value theorem gives $z(s_2) - z(s_1) = \dot z(\sigma)(s_2 - s_1) = 0$ for some $\sigma \in (s_1,s_2)$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{The geometry of the lines}

Before solving, let us understand these lines and see that they fill the whole domain without overlapping. For $y \in \mathbb{R}^n$ let
$$ L_y := \{(y + \sigma b,\ \sigma) : \sigma \geq 0\} \subset \mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty). $$
$L_y$ is the half-line which starts at the point $(y,0)$ of the initial plane and goes in the direction $(b,1)$. Along $L_y$ the time coordinate is $\sigma$ and the space coordinate is $y + \sigma b$: \emph{as time increases by $\sigma$, the space position moves by $\sigma b$.} So the lines move in space with constant velocity $b$.

\paragraph{The case $n = 1$: the slope of the lines.} Here $b$ is a number. Draw the $x$-axis horizontally and the $t$-axis vertically. The line $L_y$ is the set of points $(x,t)$ with
$$ x = y + bt, \qquad t \ge 0. $$
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $b \neq 0$, solving for $t$ gives $t = \frac{1}{b}(x - y)$: a straight line through $(y,0)$ with slope $\frac1b$. For $b > 0$ it leans to the right (as $t$ increases, $x$ increases), for $b < 0$ to the left. The larger $|b|$, the flatter the line.
    \item If $b = 0$, it is the vertical line $x = y$: then \eqref{eq:transport} says $u_t = 0$, so $u$ does not change in time.
\end{itemize}
All the lines $L_y$, $y \in \mathbb{R}$, have the \emph{same} slope, so they are parallel; they differ only in their starting point $y$ on the $x$-axis.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.1]
  % axes
  \draw[->] (-0.5,0) -- (7.6,0) node[right] {$x$};
  \draw[->] (0,-0.3) -- (0,3.6) node[above] {$t$};
  % characteristic lines x = y + b t with b = 1
  \foreach \y in {-0.5,0.5,1.5,2.5,3.5,4.5} {
    \draw[thick,blue!70!black] (\y,0) -- ({\y+3.2},3.2);
  }
  % highlighted line through (x,t)
  \draw[very thick,red!75!black] (2.5,0) -- (5.7,3.2);
  \fill[red!75!black] (4.5,2) circle (2pt) node[right=3pt] {$(x,t)$};
  \fill[red!75!black] (2.5,0) circle (2pt) node[below] {$(x - tb,\,0)$};
  % initial plane label
  \node[below] at (6.6,0) {$\Gamma = \{t = 0\}$};
  % time marker
  \draw[dashed] (0,2) -- (4.5,2);
  \node[left] at (0,2) {$t$};
  % displacement
  \draw[dashed] (4.5,2) -- (4.5,0);
  \draw[<->] (2.5,-0.55) -- (4.5,-0.55);
  \node[below] at (3.5,-0.55) {$tb$};
\end{tikzpicture}

\small The lines $x = y + bt$ for several $y$ (here $n = 1$, $b > 0$). The line through $(x,t)$ meets $t = 0$ at $x - tb$.
\end{center}

\paragraph{The general case.} The following lemma says precisely that the half-lines $L_y$ are disjoint and that together they make up the whole closed half-space.

\begin{lemma}[The lines $L_y$ fill the domain]\label{lem:transgeom}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)] Each $L_y$ meets the initial plane $\Gamma := \mathbb{R}^n \times \{t = 0\}$ in exactly one point, namely $(y,0)$.
    \item[(b)] If $y \neq y'$, then $L_y \cap L_{y'} = \emptyset$.
    \item[(c)] $\displaystyle\bigcup_{y \in \mathbb{R}^n} L_y = \mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$. More precisely, the point $(x,t)$ with $t \geq 0$ lies on the line $L_{x - tb}$, and on no other.
    \item[(d)] For $(x,t)$ with $t > 0$, the set $\{(x + sb, t + s) : s \geq -t\}$ (the part of the line through $(x,t)$ with direction $(b,1)$ which lies in $t \geq 0$) is exactly $L_{x - tb}$; it reaches $\Gamma$ when $s = -t$, at the point $(x - tb,0)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) A point $(y + \sigma b,\sigma)$ of $L_y$ lies on $\Gamma$ if and only if its time coordinate is $0$, i.e.\ $\sigma = 0$; the point is then $(y,0)$.

(b) Suppose a point lies on both, $(y + \sigma b, \sigma) = (y' + \sigma' b,\sigma')$. Comparing time coordinates, $\sigma = \sigma'$. Comparing space coordinates, $y + \sigma b = y' + \sigma b$, so $y = y'$. Hence if $y \ne y'$ there is no common point.

(c) Every $L_y$ lies in $\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$ since $\sigma \geq 0$. Conversely, given $(x,t)$ with $t \geq 0$, put $y := x - tb$ and $\sigma := t$. Then $(y + \sigma b,\sigma) = (x - tb + tb, t) = (x,t)$, so $(x,t) \in L_{x - tb}$. By (b), $(x,t)$ lies on no other $L_{y'}$.

(d) Put $\sigma = t + s$; then $s \ge -t$ if and only if $\sigma \geq 0$, and
$$ (x + sb,\ t + s) = \big((x - tb) + (t + s)b,\ t + s\big) = \big((x - tb) + \sigma b,\ \sigma\big). $$
So as $s$ runs over $[-t,\infty)$ the point runs over all of $L_{x - tb}$. The time coordinate $t + s$ vanishes for $s = -t$, where the point is $(x - tb,0)$.
\end{proof}

\emph{Why every line meets the initial plane.} The direction $(b,1)$ has time component $1 \neq 0$; so the lines are never parallel to the plane $\Gamma = \{t = 0\}$, and going backwards in time along any line we reach $t = 0$ after exactly the time $t$. (This is the \emph{transversality} of the lines to the initial plane, as in Section~\ref{sec:moc}.)

\paragraph{Summary of the idea.} Lemmas~\ref{lem:transconst} and~\ref{lem:transgeom} together say:
\begin{itemize}
    \item the closed half-space $\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$ is the union of the disjoint half-lines $L_y$, $y \in \mathbb{R}^n$;
    \item a solution $u$ is constant on each $L_y$;
    \item each $L_y$ touches the initial plane exactly once, at $(y,0)$, where the value of $u$ is the given initial value $g(y)$.
\end{itemize}
Therefore, if we know $u$ on the initial plane, we know the constant value of $u$ on each line, hence on all lines, hence everywhere. This is carried out next.

\subsubsection{Initial-value problem}

For definiteness therefore, let us consider the initial-value problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transivp}
\begin{cases} u_t + b\cdot Du = 0 & \text{in } \mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty) \\ u = g & \text{on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \{t = 0\}. \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Here $b \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $g : \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ are known, and the problem is to compute $u$. Given $(x,t)$ as above, the line through $(x,t)$ with direction $(b,1)$ is represented parametrically by $(x + sb, t + s)$ $(s \in \mathbb{R})$. This line hits the plane $\Gamma := \mathbb{R}^n \times \{t = 0\}$ when $s = -t$, at the point $(x - tb, 0)$. Since $u$ is constant on the line and $u(x - tb,0) = g(x - tb)$, we deduce
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transsol}
u(x,t) = g(x - tb) \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t \geq 0).
\end{equation}
So, \emph{if} \eqref{eq:transivp} has a sufficiently regular solution $u$, it must certainly be given by \eqref{eq:transsol}. And conversely, it is easy to check directly that if $g$ is $C^1$, then $u$ defined by \eqref{eq:transsol} is indeed a solution of \eqref{eq:transivp}.

To make this rigorous we must say what ``solution'' means. By a \emph{solution} of \eqref{eq:transivp} we mean a function $u \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)) \cap C(\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty))$ which satisfies the PDE at every point with $t > 0$ and satisfies $u(x,0) = g(x)$ for all $x$. The continuity up to $t = 0$ is what connects the values for $t > 0$ to the initial values; without it the initial condition would have no influence on $u$ for $t > 0$.

\begin{theorem}[Transport equation: uniqueness and existence]\label{thm:transport}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)] (Uniqueness) If $u$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:transivp}, then $u(x,t) = g(x - tb)$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$, $t \geq 0$.
    \item[(b)] (Existence) If $g \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n)$, then $u(x,t) := g(x - tb)$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:transivp}; in fact $u \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R})$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) Let $(x,t)$ be given. If $t = 0$, the claim is the initial condition $u(x,0) = g(x)$. Let $t > 0$ and put $z(s) := u(x + sb, t + s)$ for $s \geq -t$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item By Lemma~\ref{lem:transconst}, $z$ is constant on $(-t,\infty)$; in particular $z(s) = z(0) = u(x,t)$ for all $s \in (-t, \infty)$.
    \item $z$ is continuous on $[-t,\infty)$: it is the composition of the continuous map $s \mapsto (x + sb, t + s)$ (into $\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$) with $u$, which is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$.
    \item Hence $z(-t) = \lim_{s \to -t^+} z(s) = \lim_{s\to -t^+} u(x,t) = u(x,t)$. The limit of a constant is that constant.
    \item But $z(-t) = u(x - tb, 0) = g(x - tb)$ by the initial condition.
\end{itemize}
Therefore $u(x,t) = g(x - tb)$. Note where each hypothesis was used: the PDE (and $u \in C^1$) gives $z$ constant for $s > -t$, i.e.\ at points with positive time; the continuity of $u$ up to $t = 0$ carries this constant value down to the initial plane, at $s = -t$; and the initial condition identifies it as $g(x - tb)$.

(b) Let $g \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $u(x,t) = g(x - tb)$, i.e.\ $u(x,t) = g(x_1 - tb_1, \dots, x_n - tb_n)$. This is the composition of $g$ with the smooth map $(x,t) \mapsto x - tb$, so $u \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R})$, and by the chain rule:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The $j$-th slot $x_j - tb_j$ has $t$-derivative $-b_j$, so
    $$ u_t(x,t) = \sum_{j=1}^n g_{x_j}(x - tb)\,(-b_j) = -\,b\cdot Dg(x - tb). $$
    \item Only the $i$-th slot depends on $x_i$, with derivative $1$, so $u_{x_i}(x,t) = g_{x_i}(x - tb)$, i.e.\ $Du(x,t) = Dg(x - tb)$, and $b\cdot Du(x,t) = b\cdot Dg(x - tb)$.
\end{itemize}
Adding, $u_t + b\cdot Du = -b\cdot Dg(x - tb) + b\cdot Dg(x - tb) = 0$ everywhere. Finally $u(x,0) = g(x - 0\cdot b) = g(x)$, and $u$ is continuous. So $u$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:transivp}.
\end{proof}

\emph{Interpretation of \eqref{eq:transsol}.} For $n = 1$, $b > 0$: the graph of $u(\cdot,t)$ is the graph of $g$ shifted to the right by the distance $tb$. The initial profile $g$ is \emph{transported} with velocity $b$, without changing its shape. This is the origin of the name.

\paragraph{Remark.} If $g$ is not $C^1$, then there is obviously no $C^1$ solution of \eqref{eq:transivp}. But even in this case formula \eqref{eq:transsol} certainly provides a strong, and in fact the only reasonable, candidate for a solution. We may thus informally declare $u(x,t) = g(x - tb)$ $(x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t \ge 0)$ to be a \emph{weak solution} of \eqref{eq:transivp}, even should $g$ not be $C^1$. This all makes sense even if $g$, and thus $u$, are discontinuous. Such a notion, that a nonsmooth or even discontinuous function may sometimes solve a PDE, will come up again later.

\emph{Proof of the first sentence.} Suppose $u$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:transivp} (so $u \in C^1$ for $t > 0$). By Theorem~\ref{thm:transport}(a), $u(x,t) = g(x - tb)$; take $t = 1$ and $x = y + b$: then $g(y) = u(y + b, 1)$ for every $y \in \mathbb{R}^n$. The map $y \mapsto (y + b,1)$ is smooth with values in $\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)$, where $u$ is $C^1$. So $g$ is $C^1$, as a composition of $C^1$ maps. Hence: if $g$ is not $C^1$, no solution exists.

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsubsection{Nonhomogeneous problem}

Next let us look at the associated nonhomogeneous problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transnh}
\begin{cases} u_t + b\cdot Du = f & \text{in } \mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty) \\ u = g & \text{on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \{t = 0\}. \end{cases}
\end{equation}
As before fix $(x,t) \in \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ (with $t > 0$) and, inspired by the calculation above, set $z(s) := u(x + sb, t + s)$ for $s \in \mathbb{R}$ (with $s \geq -t$). Then
$$ \dot z(s) = Du(x + sb, t + s)\cdot b + u_t(x + sb, t + s) = f(x + sb, t + s). $$
Consequently
\begin{align*}
u(x,t) - g(x - bt) = z(0) - z(-t) &= \int_{-t}^0 \dot z(s)\, ds \\
&= \int_{-t}^0 f(x + sb, t + s)\, ds \\
&= \int_0^t f(x + (s - t)b, s)\, ds;
\end{align*}
and so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transnhsol}
u(x,t) = g(x - tb) + \int_0^t f(x + (s - t)b, s)\, ds \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t \geq 0)
\end{equation}
solves the initial-value problem \eqref{eq:transnh}.

\emph{The idea.} Along the line through $(x,t)$, $u$ is no longer constant: by the computation of $\dot z$, its rate of change along the line is the given function $f$. So the value at $(x,t)$ is the starting value $g(x - tb)$ at the foot of the line, plus the accumulated change, which is the integral of $f$ along the segment of the line from $(x - tb, 0)$ to $(x,t)$.

We now prove this rigorously. As in the homogeneous case, a \emph{solution} of \eqref{eq:transnh} means $u \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n\times(0,\infty)) \cap C(\mathbb{R}^n\times[0,\infty))$ satisfying the PDE for $t > 0$ and $u(x,0) = g(x)$. We assume $f$ is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$.

\begin{theorem}[Nonhomogeneous transport equation]\label{thm:transportnh}
Let $f \in C(\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty))$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)] (Uniqueness) If $u$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:transnh}, then $u$ is given by \eqref{eq:transnhsol}.
    \item[(b)] (Existence) If $g \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $f \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n\times[0,\infty))$ (that is, $f$ and its first partial derivatives are continuous up to $t = 0$), then $u$ defined by \eqref{eq:transnhsol} is a solution of \eqref{eq:transnh}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) Fix $(x,t)$. If $t = 0$, \eqref{eq:transnhsol} reads $u(x,0) = g(x)$, which is the initial condition. Let $t > 0$, and let $z(s) = u(x + sb, t + s)$ for $s \in [-t,0]$. We justify the four equalities of the computation.

\emph{Step 1: the derivative of $z$.} For $s \in (-t,0]$ the point $\gamma(s) = (x + sb, t + s)$ has time coordinate $t + s > 0$. Exactly as in Lemma~\ref{lem:transconst} (chain rule, $\gamma'(s) = (b,1)$),
$$ \dot z(s) = \big(u_t + b\cdot Du\big)(\gamma(s)) = f(x + sb, t + s) \qquad (-t < s \leq 0), $$
using the PDE in \eqref{eq:transnh} at the point $\gamma(s)$.

\emph{Step 2: the endpoints.} $z(0) = u(x,t)$, and $z(-t) = u(x - tb,0) = g(x - tb)$ by the initial condition. Also $z$ is continuous on $[-t,0]$ (composition of continuous maps, $u$ being continuous up to $t = 0$).

\emph{Step 3: Fundamental Theorem of Calculus.} The function $\varphi(s) := f(x + sb,t + s)$ is continuous on the closed interval $[-t,0]$ ($f$ is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty)$), hence bounded there, say $|\varphi| \leq M$. Let $-t < a < 0$. On $[a,0]$, $z$ is differentiable with continuous derivative $\dot z = \varphi$, so by the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus
$$ z(0) - z(a) = \int_a^0 \varphi(s)\, ds. $$
Now let $a \to -t^+$. The left side tends to $z(0) - z(-t)$ by continuity of $z$ at $-t$. On the right, $\big|\int_{-t}^0\varphi - \int_a^0\varphi\big| = \big|\int_{-t}^a\varphi\big| \le M(a + t) \to 0$. Hence
$$ u(x,t) - g(x - tb) = z(0) - z(-t) = \int_{-t}^0 f(x + sb, t + s)\, ds. $$
(We cannot apply the Fundamental Theorem directly on $[-t,0]$, since we do not know that $z$ is differentiable at $s = -t$: the PDE holds only for $t + s > 0$. The limit $a \to -t^+$ avoids this.)

\emph{Step 4: change of variables.} Substitute $\sigma = t + s$, so $s = \sigma - t$, $ds = d\sigma$; $s = -t$ corresponds to $\sigma = 0$ and $s = 0$ to $\sigma = t$:
$$ \int_{-t}^0 f(x + sb, t + s)\, ds = \int_0^t f(x + (\sigma - t)b, \sigma)\, d\sigma. $$
Renaming $\sigma$ as $s$ gives \eqref{eq:transnhsol}. Here $\sigma$ is the time coordinate of the point on the segment: $(x + (\sigma - t)b, \sigma)$ is the point of the line through $(x,t)$ at time $\sigma$, for $0 \le \sigma \le t$.

(b) Write $u = u_1 + w$, where $u_1(x,t) := g(x - tb)$ and
$$ w(x,t) := \int_0^t f(x + (s - t)b, s)\, ds. $$
By Theorem~\ref{thm:transport}(b), $u_1 \in C^1$, $(u_1)_t + b\cdot Du_1 = 0$ and $u_1(x,0) = g(x)$. So it remains to show: $w \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty)) \cap C(\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty))$, $w_t + b\cdot Dw = f$ and $w(x,0) = 0$. Then $u_t + b\cdot Du = 0 + f = f$ and $u(x,0) = g(x) + 0 = g(x)$.

\emph{Step 1: setting up Lemma~\ref{lem:leibniz}.} The difficulty is that $t$ appears in two places in $w$: in the upper limit and inside the integrand. We separate them. Let $p = (x,t') \in V := \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ (here $t'$ is any real number) and
$$ h(p,s) := f(x + (s - t')b,\ s) \qquad (s \geq 0), \qquad H(p,\tau) := \int_0^\tau h(p,s)\, ds. $$
Then $w(x,t) = H\big((x,t),t\big) =: H(x,t,t)$: we put the same $t$ into the integrand slot $t'$ and the upper limit $\tau$.

$h$ is continuous on $V \times [0,\infty)$ (the argument $(x + (s - t')b, s)$ has time coordinate $s \geq 0$, where $f$ is defined and continuous). Its partial derivatives in $p$ are, by the chain rule,
\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_i}(p,s) &= f_{x_i}(x + (s - t')b, s), \\
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t'}(p,s) &= \sum_{j=1}^n f_{x_j}(x + (s - t')b, s)(-b_j) = -\,b\cdot Df(x + (s - t')b, s),
\end{align*}
which are continuous because $f \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty))$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:leibniz}, $H$ is continuous on $V \times [0,\infty)$, $C^1$ on $V \times (0,\infty)$, and
\begin{gather*}
H_\tau(p,\tau) = h(p,\tau), \qquad H_{x_i}(p,\tau) = \int_0^\tau f_{x_i}(x + (s - t')b, s)\, ds, \\
H_{t'}(p,\tau) = -\int_0^\tau b\cdot Df(x + (s - t')b, s)\, ds.
\end{gather*}

\emph{Step 2: regularity of $w$.} $w(x,t) = H(x,t,t)$ is the composition of $H$ with the map $(x,t) \mapsto (x,t,t)$. For $t \ge 0$ this is continuous, so $w \in C(\mathbb{R}^n \times [0,\infty))$; for $t > 0$ both maps are $C^1$, so $w \in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n \times (0,\infty))$. Also $w(x,0) = \int_0^0 \cdots = 0$.

\emph{Step 3: the derivatives of $w$.} By the chain rule, $x_i$ enters $H(x,t,t)$ only through the slot $x_i$, while $t$ enters through both slots $t'$ and $\tau$:
$$ w_{x_i}(x,t) = H_{x_i}(x,t,t) = \int_0^t f_{x_i}(x + (s - t)b, s)\, ds, $$
$$ w_t(x,t) = H_{t'}(x,t,t) + H_\tau(x,t,t) = -\int_0^t b\cdot Df(x + (s - t)b, s)\, ds + f(x + (t - t)b, t). $$
The last term is $f(x,t)$.

\emph{Step 4: the equation.} Multiply $w_{x_i}$ by $b_i$, sum over $i$, and use linearity of the integral:
$$ b\cdot Dw(x,t) = \int_0^t b\cdot Df(x + (s - t)b, s)\, ds. $$
Adding this to $w_t$, the two integrals cancel:
$$ w_t + b\cdot Dw = f(x,t) \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\ t > 0). $$
This completes the proof.
\end{proof}

We will later employ this formula to solve the one-dimensional wave equation.

\paragraph{Remark.} Observe that we have derived our solutions \eqref{eq:transsol}, \eqref{eq:transnhsol} by in effect converting the partial differential equations into ordinary differential equations. This procedure is a special case of the method of characteristics (Section~\ref{sec:moc}).

Indeed, the ODE is the equation for $z(s)$: $\dot z = 0$ in the homogeneous case, $\dot z = f(x + sb, t + s)$ in the nonhomogeneous case. The lines $L_y$, along which we solved these ODEs, are the \emph{characteristic curves} of \eqref{eq:transport}: they solve $\frac{d}{d\sigma}(x,t) = (b,1)$, the vector of coefficients of the equation $b\cdot Du + 1\cdot u_t = f$.

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Solution for $n = 1$, d'Alembert's Formula}\label{ssec:dalembert}

We began the study of Laplace's equation and of the heat equation by searching for certain scaling invariant solutions. For the wave equation however we will instead present the (reasonably) elegant method of solving \eqref{eq:wave} first for $n = 1$ directly and then for $n \geq 2$ by the method of spherical means.

We first focus our attention on the initial-value problem for the one-dimensional wave equation in all of $\mathbb{R}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wave1d}
\begin{cases} u_{tt} - u_{xx} = 0 & \text{in } \mathbb{R} \times (0,\infty) \\ u = g,\ u_t = h & \text{on } \mathbb{R} \times \{t = 0\}, \end{cases}
\end{equation}
where $g, h$ are given. We desire to derive a formula for $u$ in terms of $g$ and $h$.

Here $g$ is the initial value of $u$ and $h$ the initial value of $u_t$: $u(x,0) = g(x)$ and $u_t(x,0) = h(x)$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.

\subsubsection{Derivation}

Throughout the derivation we suppose that $u$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:wave1d} with $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R} \times [0,\infty))$; that is, $u$ and its partial derivatives up to order two are continuous on $\mathbb{R} \times (0,\infty)$ and extend continuously to $t = 0$. We will find that $u$ \emph{must} be given by a certain formula.

\paragraph{Step 1: factoring the operator.} Let us first note the PDE in \eqref{eq:wave1d} can be ``factored'', to read
\begin{equation}\label{eq:factor}
\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Big)\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Big)u = u_{tt} - u_{xx} = 0.
\end{equation}

\emph{Verification.} First apply the right factor: $\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\big)u = u_t - u_x$. Then apply the left factor to the result:
$$ \Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Big)(u_t - u_x) = (u_{tt} - u_{xt}) + (u_{tx} - u_{xx}) = u_{tt} - u_{xx} + (u_{tx} - u_{xt}) = u_{tt} - u_{xx}. $$
In the last step we used $u_{tx} = u_{xt}$: the mixed partial derivatives of a $C^2$ function are equal. So the second-order operator $\partial_t^2 - \partial_x^2$ is the product of two first-order operators $\partial_t + \partial_x$ and $\partial_t - \partial_x$, just like $a^2 - b^2 = (a + b)(a - b)$. Each factor is a \emph{transport operator}, and we solve two transport equations, one after the other.

\paragraph{Step 2: the first transport equation.} Write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vdef}
v(x,t) := \Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Big)u(x,t).
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{eq:factor} says
$$ v_t(x,t) + v_x(x,t) = 0 \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R},\ t > 0). $$
This is a transport equation with constant coefficients. Applying formula \eqref{eq:transsol} (with $n = 1$, $b = 1$), we find
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vsol}
v(x,t) = a(x - t)
\end{equation}
for $a(x) := v(x,0)$.

\emph{How the transport equation is used here.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{The equation.} $v_t + v_x = 0$ is \eqref{eq:transport} with $n = 1$ and the vector $b$ equal to the number $1$: $u_t + b\cdot Du$ becomes $v_t + 1\cdot v_x$.
    \item \emph{The hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:transport}(a).} That theorem requires $v \in C^1(\mathbb{R} \times (0,\infty)) \cap C(\mathbb{R} \times [0,\infty))$. Since $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$, the functions $u_t$, $u_x$ have continuous first derivatives, so $v = u_t - u_x$ is $C^1$ for $t > 0$ and continuous up to $t = 0$. The initial value of $v$ is the function $a(x) := v(x,0)$. (We do not yet know $a$; we compute it in Step 4.)
    \item \emph{The conclusion.} Theorem~\ref{thm:transport}(a) gives $v(x,t) = a(x - t\cdot 1) = a(x - t)$.
    \item \emph{The geometry.} The lines of Lemma~\ref{lem:transgeom} have direction $(b,1) = (1,1)$: they are the lines $x - t = \text{const}$, of slope $1$ in the $(x,t)$-plane. $v$ is constant along each of them. The line through $(x,t)$ meets $t = 0$ at the point $x - t$, and there $v = a(x - t)$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Step 3: the second transport equation.} Combining now \eqref{eq:factor}--\eqref{eq:vsol}, we obtain
$$ u_t(x,t) - u_x(x,t) = a(x - t) \qquad \text{in } \mathbb{R} \times (0,\infty). $$
This is a nonhomogeneous transport equation; and so formula \eqref{eq:transnhsol} (with $n = 1$, $b = -1$, $f(x,t) = a(x - t)$) implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:u7}
\begin{aligned}
u(x,t) &= \int_0^t a(x + (t - s) - s)\, ds + b(x + t) \\
&= \frac12\int_{x-t}^{x+t} a(y)\, dy + b(x + t),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where we have $b(x) := u(x,0)$.

\emph{A warning about notation.} In \eqref{eq:u7} the letter $b$ denotes the \emph{function} $b(x) := u(x,0)$, the initial value of $u$. It has nothing to do with the vector (here: number) $b$ in the transport equation, which in this step equals $-1$.

\emph{How the nonhomogeneous transport equation is used here.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{The equation.} $u_t - u_x = a(x - t)$ is \eqref{eq:transnh} with $n = 1$, the coefficient $b = -1$ (since $u_t + (-1)u_x = u_t - u_x$), and right-hand side $f(x,t) = a(x - t)$.
    \item \emph{The hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:transportnh}(a).} We need $u \in C^1(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)) \cap C(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$ (true, since $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$) and $f$ continuous on $\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)$: $a = v(\cdot,0)$ is continuous because $v$ is continuous up to $t = 0$, so $f(x,t) = a(x - t)$ is continuous. The initial value of $u$ is the function $x \mapsto u(x,0)$, which we call $b(x)$.
    \item \emph{The formula.} \eqref{eq:transnhsol} with coefficient $-1$ reads
    $$ u(x,t) = \underbrace{u(x - t(-1),\, 0)}_{=\, b(x + t)} + \int_0^t f\big(x + (s - t)(-1),\ s\big)\, ds = b(x + t) + \int_0^t f(x + t - s,\ s)\, ds. $$
    Now $f(x + t - s, s) = a\big((x + t - s) - s\big) = a(x + (t - s) - s)$. This is the first line of \eqref{eq:u7}.
    \item \emph{The geometry.} Here the lines have direction $(-1,1)$: they are the lines $x + t = \text{const}$, of slope $-1$. The line through $(x,t)$ meets $t = 0$ at the point $x + t$; this is why $b(x + t) = u(x + t, 0)$ appears. The integral is taken along the segment of this line from $(x + t,0)$ up to $(x,t)$: at time $s$ the segment passes through the point $(x + t - s,\ s)$.
\end{itemize}

\emph{The change of variables} (second line of \eqref{eq:u7}). Substitute $y = x + t - 2s$. Then $dy = -2\,ds$, i.e.\ $ds = -\frac12 dy$; $s = 0$ gives $y = x + t$, and $s = t$ gives $y = x - t$. So
$$ \int_0^t a(x + t - 2s)\, ds = \int_{x+t}^{x-t} a(y)\Big(-\frac12\Big)dy = \frac12\int_{x-t}^{x+t} a(y)\, dy, $$
where reversing the limits of integration removed the minus sign.

\emph{Geometric meaning of $y = x + t - 2s$.} The point $(x + t - s,\, s)$ on the slope $-1$ segment carries the value $f = a\big((x + t - s) - s\big)$. By Step 2, $a(y)$ is the value of $v$ at the foot of the slope $+1$ line through that point, and that foot is $y = (x + t - s) - s = x + t - 2s$. As $s$ goes from $0$ to $t$, the foot $y$ sweeps the whole interval $[x - t, x + t]$. So the value $u(x,t)$ depends only on the initial data on the interval $[x - t, x + t]$: the base of the triangle cut out of the initial line by the two lines through $(x,t)$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.15]
  \draw[->] (-0.4,0) -- (8.4,0) node[right] {$x$};
  \draw[->] (0,-0.3) -- (0,3.5) node[above] {$t$};
  % triangle
  \fill[gray!12] (1.5,0) -- (4.5,3) -- (7.5,0) -- cycle;
  % slope +1 line through (x,t): v-line
  \draw[very thick,blue!70!black] (1.5,0) -- (4.5,3);
  % slope -1 line through (x,t): u-line
  \draw[very thick,red!75!black] (7.5,0) -- (4.5,3);
  \fill (4.5,3) circle (2pt) node[above] {$(x,t)$};
  \fill (1.5,0) circle (2pt) node[below] {$x - t$};
  \fill (7.5,0) circle (2pt) node[below] {$x + t$};
  % a point at time s on the red segment
  \fill[red!75!black] (6.3,1.2) circle (2pt) node[right=2pt] {$(x + t - s,\, s)$};
  % its slope +1 line down to y = x+t-2s
  \draw[thick,dashed,blue!70!black] (6.3,1.2) -- (5.1,0);
  \fill (5.1,0) circle (2pt) node[below] {$y = x + t - 2s$};
  \node[blue!70!black] at (2.4,1.9) {$x - t = $ const};
  \node[red!75!black] at (7.2,1.9) {$x + t = $ const};
\end{tikzpicture}

\small The two lines through $(x,t)$: slope $+1$ (where $v$ is constant) and slope $-1$ (along which we integrate). From each point of the slope $-1$ segment, the slope $+1$ line goes down to a point $y \in [x - t, x + t]$.
\end{center}

\paragraph{Step 4: using the initial conditions.} We lastly invoke the initial conditions in \eqref{eq:wave1d} to compute $a$ and $b$. The first initial condition in \eqref{eq:wave1d} gives
$$ b(x) = g(x) \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}); $$
whereas the second initial condition and \eqref{eq:vdef} imply
$$ a(x) = v(x,0) = u_t(x,0) - u_x(x,0) = h(x) - g'(x) \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R}). $$

\emph{Details.} $b(x) = u(x,0) = g(x)$ is the definition of $b$ together with the first initial condition. For $a$: $u_t(x,0) = h(x)$ is the second initial condition. And $u_x(x,0) = g'(x)$: the derivative $u_x$ at a point $(x,0)$ is a derivative \emph{along} the line $t = 0$, where $u(\cdot,0) = g$; so $u_x(x,0) = \frac{d}{dx}u(x,0) = g'(x)$. (In particular $g$ must be differentiable; this is part of the regularity we assumed.)

\paragraph{Step 5: d'Alembert's formula.} Our substituting into \eqref{eq:u7} now yields
$$ u(x,t) = \frac12\int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y) - g'(y)\, dy + g(x + t). $$
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dalembert}
u(x,t) = \frac12\big[g(x + t) + g(x - t)\big] + \frac12\int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y)\, dy \qquad (x \in \mathbb{R},\ t \geq 0).
\end{equation}
This is \emph{d'Alembert's formula}.

\emph{Details.} Split the integral and use the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus for the $g'$ term:
$$ \frac12\int_{x-t}^{x+t} g'(y)\, dy = \frac12\big[g(x + t) - g(x - t)\big]. $$
Therefore
$$ u(x,t) = \frac12\int_{x-t}^{x+t}h(y)\, dy - \frac12 g(x + t) + \frac12 g(x - t) + g(x + t) = \frac12 g(x + t) + \frac12 g(x - t) + \frac12\int_{x-t}^{x+t}h(y)\, dy. $$

\emph{Summary of the derivation.} We have shown: \emph{if} $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$ solves \eqref{eq:wave1d}, then $u$ is given by \eqref{eq:dalembert}. In particular \eqref{eq:wave1d} has \emph{at most one} such solution (uniqueness). The transport equation was used twice: the homogeneous one ($b = 1$) for $v$, and then the nonhomogeneous one ($b = -1$) for $u$.

\subsubsection{The theorem}

We have derived formula \eqref{eq:dalembert} assuming $u$ is a (sufficiently smooth) solution of \eqref{eq:wave1d}. We need to check that this really is a solution.

\bigskip
\textbf{Theorem 1 (Solution of wave equation, $n = 1$).} \textit{Assume $g \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$, $h \in C^1(\mathbb{R})$, and define $u$ by d'Alembert's formula \eqref{eq:dalembert}. Then}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(i)] $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R} \times [0,\infty))$,
    \item[(ii)] $u_{tt} - u_{xx} = 0$ \textit{in} $\mathbb{R} \times (0,\infty)$,
\end{enumerate}
\textit{and}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(iii)] $\displaystyle\lim_{\substack{(x,t)\to(x^0,0)\\ t>0}} u(x,t) = g(x^0), \qquad \lim_{\substack{(x,t)\to(x^0,0)\\ t>0}} u_t(x,t) = h(x^0)$
\end{enumerate}
\textit{for each point $x^0 \in \mathbb{R}$.}

The proof is a straightforward calculation.

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: rewriting $u$ with an antiderivative of $h$.} Let
$$ H(z) := \int_0^z h(y)\, dy \qquad (z \in \mathbb{R}). $$
By the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, $H' = h$; since $h \in C^1$, we get $H'' = h'$ continuous, so $H \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$. Moreover $\int_{x-t}^{x+t}h(y)\,dy = H(x + t) - H(x - t)$. Hence
$$ u(x,t) = \frac12\big[g(x + t) + g(x - t)\big] + \frac12\big[H(x + t) - H(x - t)\big]. $$
This formula makes sense for all $(x,t) \in \mathbb{R}^2$, and $u$ is built from the $C^2$ functions $g, H$ composed with the linear maps $(x,t) \mapsto x + t$ and $(x,t) \mapsto x - t$.

\emph{Step 2: the derivatives.} By the chain rule, $\frac{\partial}{\partial t}$ of $x + t$ is $1$, of $x - t$ is $-1$, and $\frac{\partial}{\partial x}$ of both is $1$. So
\begin{align*}
u_t &= \tfrac12\big[g'(x + t) - g'(x - t)\big] + \tfrac12\big[h(x + t) + h(x - t)\big], \\
u_x &= \tfrac12\big[g'(x + t) + g'(x - t)\big] + \tfrac12\big[h(x + t) - h(x - t)\big], \\
u_{tt} &= \tfrac12\big[g''(x + t) + g''(x - t)\big] + \tfrac12\big[h'(x + t) - h'(x - t)\big], \\
u_{xx} &= \tfrac12\big[g''(x + t) + g''(x - t)\big] + \tfrac12\big[h'(x + t) - h'(x - t)\big], \\
u_{xt} &= u_{tx} = \tfrac12\big[g''(x + t) - g''(x - t)\big] + \tfrac12\big[h'(x + t) + h'(x - t)\big].
\end{align*}
(For $u_t$ we used $H' = h$; differentiating $-H(x - t)$ in $t$ gives $-h(x - t)\cdot(-1) = +h(x - t)$. Note the sign changes: each $t$-derivative of a function of $x - t$ produces a factor $-1$, and two of them give $+1$.)

\emph{Proof of (i).} All the derivatives listed are compositions of the continuous functions $g, g', g'', h, h'$ with continuous maps, so they are continuous on all of $\mathbb{R}^2$. Hence $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R}^2)$, and in particular $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$.

\emph{Proof of (ii).} The expressions for $u_{tt}$ and $u_{xx}$ are identical, so $u_{tt} - u_{xx} = 0$ at every point, in particular in $\mathbb{R} \times (0,\infty)$.

\emph{Proof of (iii).} Since $u$ and $u_t$ are continuous on $\mathbb{R}^2$, their limits as $(x,t) \to (x^0,0)$ (from $t > 0$ or otherwise) are their values at $(x^0,0)$. Putting $t = 0$ in the formulas:
$$ u(x^0,0) = \tfrac12\big[g(x^0) + g(x^0)\big] + \tfrac12\big[H(x^0) - H(x^0)\big] = g(x^0), $$
$$ u_t(x^0,0) = \tfrac12\big[g'(x^0) - g'(x^0)\big] + \tfrac12\big[h(x^0) + h(x^0)\big] = h(x^0). $$
\end{proof}

\emph{Existence and uniqueness together.} If $g \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$ and $h \in C^1(\mathbb{R})$, the Theorem gives a solution $u \in C^2(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$ of \eqref{eq:wave1d}, and the derivation shows that every solution in this class is given by \eqref{eq:dalembert}. So \eqref{eq:wave1d} has exactly one solution in $C^2(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$, namely d'Alembert's.

\paragraph{Remarks.}

(i) In view of \eqref{eq:dalembert}, our solution $u$ has the form
$$ u(x,t) = F(x + t) + G(x - t) $$
for appropriate functions $F$ and $G$. Conversely any function of this form solves $u_{tt} - u_{xx} = 0$. Hence the general solution of the one-dimensional wave equation is a sum of the general solution of $u_t - u_x = 0$ and the general solution of $u_t + u_x = 0$. This is a consequence of the factorization \eqref{eq:factor}.

\emph{Explanation.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{The form.} With $H$ as in the proof, $F(z) := \frac12 g(z) + \frac12 H(z)$ and $G(z) := \frac12 g(z) - \frac12 H(z)$ give $F(x + t) + G(x - t) = u(x,t)$.
    \item \emph{Conversely}, if $F, G \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$ and $u(x,t) = F(x + t) + G(x - t)$, then by the chain rule $u_{tt} = F''(x + t) + G''(x - t) = u_{xx}$.
    \item \emph{The two pieces.} $F(x + t)$ solves $u_t - u_x = 0$, since $\partial_t F(x + t) = F'(x + t) = \partial_x F(x + t)$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:transport}, the $C^1$ solutions of $u_t - u_x = 0$ (coefficient $b = -1$) are exactly the functions $F(x + t)$, constant along the lines $x + t = $ const. Similarly $G(x - t)$ solves $u_t + u_x = 0$ ($b = 1$), and these are all its solutions; they are constant along the lines $x - t = $ const.
    \item \emph{Why the factorization explains this.} The two factors commute: $(\partial_t + \partial_x)(\partial_t - \partial_x) = (\partial_t - \partial_x)(\partial_t + \partial_x) = \partial_t^2 - \partial_x^2$ (for $C^2$ functions, by equality of mixed partials). So anything killed by $\partial_t - \partial_x$ is killed by the product, and anything killed by $\partial_t + \partial_x$ is killed by the product; by linearity so is their sum. The derivation shows conversely that every $C^2$ solution is such a sum: a wave $F$ moving to the left with speed one plus a wave $G$ moving to the right with speed one.
\end{itemize}

(ii) We see from \eqref{eq:dalembert} that if $g \in C^k$ and $h \in C^{k-1}$, then $u \in C^k$, but is not in general smoother. Thus the wave equation does \emph{not} cause instantaneous smoothing of the initial data, as does the heat equation.

\emph{Explanation.} If $g \in C^k$ and $h \in C^{k-1}$ ($k \geq 1$), then $H \in C^k$ (as $H' = h$), and $u = \frac12[g(x+t) + g(x-t)] + \frac12[H(x+t) - H(x-t)]$ is $C^k$ by the chain rule. On the other hand, take $h \equiv 0$ and $g \in C^k$ which is not $C^{k+1}$ (for example $g(x) = x^k|x|$, whose $k$-th derivative is a multiple of $|x|$, which is not differentiable at $0$). Then $u(x,t) = \frac12[g(x + t) + g(x - t)]$. If $u$ were $C^{k+1}$, then so would be $g(x) = u(x,0)$, the restriction of $u$ to the line $t = 0$, a contradiction. So the solution is not smoother than the data, at any time. Compare Theorem 1(i) of Section~\ref{sec:heat}: there $u \in C^\infty$ for $t > 0$ even if $g$ is only continuous and bounded.


\subsubsection{The wave equation with speed $c$}\label{sssec:wavec}

In problems one often meets the wave equation in the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wavec}
\begin{cases} u_{tt} - c^2u_{xx} = 0 & x \in \mathbb{R},\ t > 0 \\ u(x,0) = g(x),\ u_t(x,0) = h(x) & x \in \mathbb{R}, \end{cases}
\end{equation}
where $c > 0$ is a constant (the speed of the waves). Its solution is \emph{d'Alembert's formula with speed $c$}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dalembertc}
\boxed{\ u(x,t) = \frac12\big[g(x + ct) + g(x - ct)\big] + \frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct} h(y)\, dy\ }
\end{equation}
For $c = 1$ this is \eqref{eq:dalembert}. Theorem 1 holds verbatim for \eqref{eq:wavec} and \eqref{eq:dalembertc} (with $u_{tt} - c^2u_{xx}$ in (ii)): if $g \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$, $h \in C^1(\mathbb{R})$, then \eqref{eq:dalembertc} is the unique solution in $C^2(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))$.

\emph{How to get it.} There is nothing new to derive; either of the following reduces it to what we have done.
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{Same derivation.} Factor $u_{tt} - c^2u_{xx} = \big(\partial_t + c\,\partial_x\big)\big(\partial_t - c\,\partial_x\big)u$ and repeat Steps 1--5 with the transport coefficients $b = c$ and then $b = -c$ in place of $1$ and $-1$. The lines are now $x \mp ct = $ const (slopes $\pm\frac1c$), they meet $t = 0$ at $x - ct$ and $x + ct$, and the substitution $y = x + ct - 2cs$ produces the factor $\frac{1}{2c}$.
    \item \emph{Rescaling time.} Put $w(x,\tau) := u\big(x, \frac{\tau}{c}\big)$, i.e.\ $u(x,t) = w(x,ct)$. By the chain rule $w_\tau = \frac1c u_t$ and $w_{\tau\tau} = \frac{1}{c^2}u_{tt} = u_{xx} = w_{xx}$. So $w$ solves the wave equation with speed $1$, with $w(x,0) = g(x)$ and $w_\tau(x,0) = \frac1c h(x)$. By \eqref{eq:dalembert},
    $$ w(x,\tau) = \frac12\big[g(x + \tau) + g(x - \tau)\big] + \frac12\int_{x-\tau}^{x+\tau}\frac{h(y)}{c}\, dy, $$
    and putting $\tau = ct$ gives \eqref{eq:dalembertc}.
\end{itemize}

As in Remark (i), every solution has the form $u(x,t) = F(x + ct) + G(x - ct)$: a wave moving to the left and a wave moving to the right, both with speed $c$. The value $u(x,t)$ depends only on the initial data on the interval $[x - ct,\, x + ct]$.


\subsubsection{Domain of Dependence, Range of Influence and Domain of Determinacy}\label{sssec:dod}

We now make some observations based on d'Alembert's formula \eqref{eq:dalembertc}. Throughout, $c > 0$, $g \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$, $h \in C^1(\mathbb{R})$, and $u$ is the solution of \eqref{eq:wavec}, so
$$ u(x,t) = \frac12\big[g(x + ct) + g(x - ct)\big] + \frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct} h(y)\, dy. $$
The basic question is: \emph{which initial data does the value $u(x,t)$ use, and which values of $u$ does a piece of initial data affect?} The answers are read off from the formula, and are described by two families of straight lines in the $(x,t)$-plane, the \emph{characteristics}
$$ x - ct = \text{const} \ \ (\text{slope } \tfrac1c,\ \text{up to the right}), \qquad x + ct = \text{const} \ \ (\text{slope } -\tfrac1c,\ \text{up to the left}). $$
These are the lines of the two transport equations in the factorization $(\partial_t + c\partial_x)(\partial_t - c\partial_x)u = 0$. In all pictures below we take $t$ upwards; the initial line $t = 0$ is the $x$-axis.

%-----------------------------
\paragraph{1. Domain of dependence of a point.}

\begin{definition}[Domain of dependence]\label{def:dod}
Let $(x,t)$ with $t > 0$. The interval
$$ [x - ct,\ x + ct] $$
on the initial line $t = 0$ is called the \emph{domain of dependence} (of the solution) at $(x,t)$.
\end{definition}

\emph{Meaning.} The value of the solution $u$ at $(x,t)$, $t > 0$, depends on the values of the initial data only in the interval $[x - ct, x + ct]$. Indeed, in d'Alembert's formula, $g$ is evaluated only at the two points $x \pm ct$, and $h$ is integrated only over $[x - ct, x + ct]$. Nothing outside this interval enters.

\emph{Geometry.} Draw the two characteristics through $(x,t)$ backwards in time. The line $x' - ct' = x - ct$ (slope $\frac1c$) meets $t' = 0$ at $x - ct$; the line $x' + ct' = x + ct$ (slope $-\frac1c$) meets $t' = 0$ at $x + ct$. Together with the $x$-axis they form a triangle with top vertex $(x,t)$; the domain of dependence is its base. The base has length $2ct$: the later the time $t$, the longer the interval of initial data on which $u(x,t)$ depends.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:dod}
Let $u_1$, $u_2$ be the solutions with data $(g_1,h_1)$, $(g_2,h_2)$. If $g_1 = g_2$ and $h_1 = h_2$ on $[x - ct, x + ct]$, then $u_1(x,t) = u_2(x,t)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By d'Alembert's formula, $u_1(x,t) - u_2(x,t) = \frac12[(g_1 - g_2)(x + ct) + (g_1 - g_2)(x - ct)] + \frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct}(h_1 - h_2)(y)\, dy$. The points $x \pm ct$ lie in the interval, and the integral runs over the interval, where $g_1 - g_2$ and $h_1 - h_2$ vanish. So the difference is $0$.
\end{proof}

The interval cannot be made smaller: if $[\alpha,\beta] \subset [x - ct, x + ct]$ with $\alpha < \beta$, taking $g = 0$ and $h \ge 0$ a $C^1$ bump which is positive on $(\alpha,\beta)$ and zero elsewhere gives $u(x,t) = \frac{1}{2c}\int_\alpha^\beta h > 0$, while for $h = 0$ we get $u(x,t) = 0$. So the data at every part of the interval does matter.

%-----------------------------
\paragraph{2. Range of influence of a point.}

\begin{definition}[Range of influence of a point]\label{def:roi}
Let $y \in \mathbb{R}$. The set
$$ \big\{(x,t) : t > 0,\ x \in [y - ct,\ y + ct]\big\} = \big\{(x,t) : t > 0,\ |x - y| \leq ct\big\} $$
is called the \emph{range of influence} of the point $(y,0)$. At a fixed time $t > 0$ it is the line segment $\{(x,t) : x \in [y - ct, y + ct]\}$.
\end{definition}

\emph{Meaning.} The initial data at the point $y$ can influence the value of $u$ at time $t > 0$ only on the segment $[y - ct, y + ct]$. This is the reverse of the previous question: there we fixed $(x,t)$ and asked which $y$ matter; here we fix $y$ and ask which $(x,t)$ it reaches.

\emph{The two notions are dual.} For $t > 0$:
$$ y \in [x - ct, x + ct] \iff |x - y| \leq ct \iff x \in [y - ct, y + ct]. $$
That is: \emph{$(x,t)$ lies in the range of influence of $(y,0)$ if and only if $y$ lies in the domain of dependence of $(x,t)$.}

\emph{Geometry.} From $(y,0)$ draw the two characteristics forward in time: $x - ct = y$ (to the right) and $x + ct = y$ (to the left). The range of influence is the wedge (an upside-down triangle, opening upwards) between them. At time $t$ its width is $2ct$: the influence of $(y,0)$ spreads to the left and to the right with speed exactly $c$.

\begin{lemma}[Finite speed of propagation]\label{lem:roi}
Let $\varepsilon > 0$, and suppose the data $(g_1,h_1)$ and $(g_2,h_2)$ agree outside $(y - \varepsilon, y + \varepsilon)$. Then $u_1(x,t) = u_2(x,t)$ whenever $|x - y| \geq ct + \varepsilon$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $|x - y| \ge ct + \varepsilon$ and $z \in [x - ct, x + ct]$. Then $|z - y| \geq |x - y| - |z - x| \geq ct + \varepsilon - ct = \varepsilon$, so $z \notin (y - \varepsilon, y + \varepsilon)$. Thus the data agree on the domain of dependence $[x - ct, x + ct]$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:dod} gives $u_1(x,t) = u_2(x,t)$.
\end{proof}
So a change of the data near $y$ is felt only in (a slightly widened version of) the range of influence of $(y,0)$; letting $\varepsilon \to 0$ we recover the wedge $|x - y| \le ct$. Compare the heat equation, where a change of the data anywhere changes $u(x,t)$ at every $x$, for every $t > 0$ (Remark (ii) after Theorem 1 of Section~\ref{sec:heat}).

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95]
  % left: domain of dependence
  \begin{scope}
    \draw[->] (-0.3,0) -- (5,0) node[right] {$x$};
    \draw[->] (0,-0.2) -- (0,2.8) node[above] {$t$};
    \fill[gray!15] (0.8,0) -- (2.4,1.6) -- (4,0) -- cycle;
    \draw[thick] (0.8,0) -- (2.4,1.6) -- (4,0);
    \draw[very thick,blue!70!black] (0.8,0) -- (4,0);
    \fill (2.4,1.6) circle (1.8pt) node[above] {$(x,t)$};
    \node[below] at (0.8,0) {$x - ct$};
    \node[below] at (4,0) {$x + ct$};
    \node at (2.4,-0.9) {\small domain of dependence of $(x,t)$};
  \end{scope}
  % right: range of influence of a point

    \draw[->] (-0.6,-0.2) -- (-0.6,2.8) node[above] {$t$};
    \fill[gray!15] (2.4,0) -- (0.6,1.8) -- (4.2,1.8) -- cycle;
    \draw[thick] (0.6,1.8) -- (2.4,0) -- (4.2,1.8);
    \draw[dashed] (-0.6,1.8) -- (4.7,1.8);
    \draw[very thick,red!75!black] (0.6,1.8) -- (4.2,1.8);
    \node[left] at (-0.6,1.8) {$t$};
    \node[above] at (0.6,1.8) {\small $y - ct$};
    \node[above] at (4.2,1.8) {\small $y + ct$};
    \fill (2.4,0) circle (1.8pt) node[below] {$(y,0)$};
    \node at (2.4,-0.9) {\small range of influence of $(y,0)$};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

%-----------------------------
\paragraph{3. Range of influence of an interval.}

\begin{definition}[Range of influence of an interval]\label{def:roiab}
Let $a < b$. The set
$$ \big\{(x,t) : t > 0,\ x \in [a - ct,\ b + ct]\big\} $$
is called the \emph{range of influence} of the interval $[a,b]$ (on the initial line $t = 0$).
\end{definition}

\emph{Meaning.} The initial values in the interval $[a,b]$ on $t = 0$ will influence the values of the solution $u$ only in this region.

\emph{It is the union of the ranges of influence of the points of $[a,b]$.} For $t > 0$, $(x,t)$ lies in the range of influence of some $(y,0)$, $y \in [a,b]$
\begin{align*}
&\iff \text{there is } y \in [a,b] \text{ with } y \in [x - ct, x + ct] \\
&\iff [x - ct, x + ct] \cap [a,b] \neq \emptyset \\
&\iff x - ct \leq b \ \text{ and } \ x + ct \geq a \\
&\iff a - ct \leq x \leq b + ct.
\end{align*}
(Two closed intervals intersect exactly when each one's left end is at most the other's right end.) So the range of influence of $[a,b]$ is the set of points whose domain of dependence \emph{meets} $[a,b]$.

\emph{Geometry.} This is the region between the \emph{diverging} characteristics $x + ct = a$ drawn from the point $(a,0)$ (going up to the left) and $x - ct = b$ drawn from $(b,0)$ (going up to the right). At time $t$ it is the segment $[a - ct, b + ct]$: the interval $[a,b]$ enlarged by $ct$ on each side.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:roiab}
If the data $(g_1,h_1)$ and $(g_2,h_2)$ agree outside $[a,b]$, then $u_1(x,t) = u_2(x,t)$ for all $t > 0$ with $x + ct < a$ or $x - ct > b$, i.e.\ outside the range of influence of $[a,b]$. In particular, if $g$ and $h$ vanish outside $[a,b]$, then $u(\cdot,t)$ vanishes outside $[a - ct, b + ct]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $x + ct < a$, the whole domain of dependence $[x - ct, x + ct]$ lies to the left of $a$; if $x - ct > b$, it lies to the right of $b$. Either way it does not meet $[a,b]$, so the data agree on it, and Lemma~\ref{lem:dod} applies. For the second statement compare with the zero data, whose solution is $u \equiv 0$.
\end{proof}

%-----------------------------
\paragraph{4. Domain of determinacy of an interval.}

On the other hand, the \emph{converging} characteristics emanating from $(a,0)$ and $(b,0)$, namely $x - ct = a$ (going up to the right from $a$) and $x + ct = b$ (going up to the left from $b$), meet when $t = \frac{1}{2c}(b - a)$. Indeed, subtracting the two equations gives $2ct = b - a$, and adding them gives $2x = a + b$. So they meet at the point $\big(\frac{a+b}{2},\ \frac{b-a}{2c}\big)$, above the midpoint of $[a,b]$.

\begin{definition}[Domain of determinacy]\label{def:dodet}
Let $a < b$. The closed triangle with vertices
$$ (a,0), \qquad (b,0), \qquad \Big(\frac{a+b}{2},\ \frac{b-a}{2c}\Big), $$
that is, the set
$$ \big\{(x,t) : t \geq 0,\ a + ct \leq x \leq b - ct\big\}, $$
is called the \emph{domain of determinacy} of the interval $[a,b]$.
\end{definition}

\emph{Meaning.} The values of the solution $u$ in this triangle are determined by the initial values within the interval $[a,b]$ at $t = 0$ alone: if we know $g$ and $h$ on $[a,b]$ (and nothing about them outside), we know $u$ everywhere in the triangle.

\emph{It is the set of points whose domain of dependence lies inside $[a,b]$.} For $t \ge 0$,
$$ [x - ct, x + ct] \subset [a,b] \iff a \leq x - ct \ \text{ and } \ x + ct \leq b \iff a + ct \leq x \leq b - ct. $$
Such $x$ exist only if $a + ct \leq b - ct$, i.e.\ $t \leq \frac{b - a}{2c}$; at that time the interval shrinks to the single point $x = \frac{a+b}{2}$. The two inequalities say: $(x,t)$ lies to the right of the line $x - ct = a$ and to the left of the line $x + ct = b$. This is the triangle described above. Its two slanted sides are the converging characteristics; its base is $[a,b]$; its height is $\frac{b-a}{2c}$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:dodet}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)] If the data $(g_1,h_1)$ and $(g_2,h_2)$ agree on $[a,b]$, then $u_1 = u_2$ in the domain of determinacy of $[a,b]$.
    \item[(b)] The domain of determinacy is the largest set with this property: if $t > 0$ and $(x,t)$ is not in it, there are data which agree on $[a,b]$ but give different values at $(x,t)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(a) If $(x,t)$ lies in the triangle, its domain of dependence $[x - ct, x + ct]$ lies in $[a,b]$, where the data agree; Lemma~\ref{lem:dod} gives $u_1(x,t) = u_2(x,t)$. (For $t = 0$ simply $u_i(x,0) = g_i(x)$ with $x \in [a,b]$.)

(b) If $(x,t)$ is not in the triangle, then $x + ct > b$ or $x - ct < a$. Say $x + ct > b$ (the other case is symmetric). The interval $(\max\{b, x - ct\},\ x + ct)$ is nonempty and lies inside the domain of dependence but outside $[a,b]$. Take $g_1 = g_2 = 0$, $h_1 = 0$, and $h_2 \geq 0$ a $C^1$ bump which is positive on this interval and zero elsewhere. Then the data agree on $[a,b]$, $u_1(x,t) = 0$, and $u_2(x,t) = \frac{1}{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct}h_2 > 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}

    \draw[->] (-0.6,-0.2) -- (-0.6,2.8) node[above] {$t$};
    \fill[gray!15] (2,0) -- (0.6,1.4) -- (5.2,1.4) -- (3.8,0) -- cycle;

    \draw[very thick,blue!70!black] (0.8,0) -- (4.4,0);

    \node[below] at (0.8,0) {$a$};
    \node[below] at (4.4,0) {$b$};

    \node[anchor=west] at (3.9,0.8) {\small $x + ct = b$};

  \end{scope}
