%-----------------------------
\paragraph{Summary.} Everything is decided by the domain of dependence $[x - ct, x + ct]$ of each point $(x,t)$, $t > 0$:
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{|p{4.6cm}|p{3.4cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{Notion} & \textbf{Set} & \textbf{Description} \\ \hline
domain of dependence of $(x,t)$ & $[x - ct,\, x + ct]$ & data used by $u(x,t)$ \\ \hline
range of influence of $(y,0)$ & $|x - y| \leq ct$ & points $(x,t)$ whose domain of dependence contains $y$ \\ \hline
range of influence of $[a,b]$ & $a - ct \leq x \leq b + ct$ & points whose domain of dependence \emph{meets} $[a,b]$ \\ \hline
domain of determinacy of $[a,b]$ & $a + ct \leq x \leq b - ct$ & points whose domain of dependence \emph{lies in} $[a,b]$ \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{itemize}
    \item The domain of dependence looks \emph{backwards} from a point $(x,t)$ to the initial line; the ranges of influence look \emph{forwards} from the initial line.
    \item The range of influence of $[a,b]$ is bounded by the \emph{diverging} characteristics from $a$ and $b$ and grows with $t$; the domain of determinacy is bounded by the \emph{converging} characteristics and ends at the time $\frac{b-a}{2c}$.
    \item The domain of determinacy lies inside the range of influence ($a + ct \le x \le b - ct$ implies $a - ct \leq x \leq b + ct$). In the triangle the data on $[a,b]$ decides $u$ completely; in the rest of the range of influence the data on $[a,b]$ affects $u$, but data outside $[a,b]$ affects it too; outside the range of influence the data on $[a,b]$ has no effect at all.
\end{itemize}

%=====================================================================
\newpage
\section{Classification of Second-Order PDE}\label{sec:classify}

The three equations we studied,
$$ \Delta u = u_{xx} + u_{yy} = 0, \qquad u_t - u_{xx} = 0, \qquad u_{tt} - u_{xx} = 0, $$
behave very differently: harmonic functions are smooth and satisfy mean-value and maximum principles; solutions of the heat equation become smooth instantly, and become positive everywhere at once from nonnegative data; solutions of the wave equation are not smoothed, and propagate with finite speed along characteristic lines. In this section we see that \emph{every} second-order linear equation in two variables is, after a suitable change of variables, of one of these three types (up to lower-order terms). The type is decided by a simple sign condition on the coefficients of the second derivatives.

\subsection{Standard form}

A general linear or semi-linear second-order partial differential equation in two independent variables $(x,y)$ is written in the standard form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pde2}
R\,u_{xx} + S\,u_{xy} + T\,u_{yy} + f(x,y,u,u_x,u_y) = 0,
\end{equation}
in an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^2$, where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $R$, $S$ and $T$ are the coefficients of the highest-order derivatives ($u_{xx}$, $u_{xy}$, $u_{yy}$ respectively);
    \item these coefficients can be constants or functions of $x$ and $y$ (we assume they are $C^1$ functions, not all zero at any point);
    \item $f(x,y,u,u_x,u_y)$ contains all the lower-order terms (first derivatives and the function itself).
\end{itemize}
The part $R\,u_{xx} + S\,u_{xy} + T\,u_{yy}$ is called the \emph{principal part}. We always assume $u \in C^2(\Omega)$, so that $u_{xy} = u_{yx}$; this is why there is only one mixed term.

(Some books write the principal part as $a u_{xx} + 2b u_{xy} + c u_{yy}$; then $R = a$, $S = 2b$, $T = c$ and $S^2 - 4RT = 4(b^2 - ac)$.)

\subsection{Classification using $R$, $S$, $T$}

\begin{definition}[Type of a second-order equation]\label{def:type}
The \emph{discriminant} of \eqref{eq:pde2} at a point $(x,y)$ is
$$ \Delta(x,y) := S^2 - 4RT. $$
Equation \eqref{eq:pde2} is called, at the point $(x,y)$,
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{hyperbolic} if $S^2 - 4RT > 0$,
    \item \textbf{parabolic} if $S^2 - 4RT = 0$,
    \item \textbf{elliptic} if $S^2 - 4RT < 0$.
\end{itemize}
It is called hyperbolic (parabolic, elliptic) in $\Omega$ if it is so at every point of $\Omega$.
\end{definition}

\emph{The model equations} (write $y$ for $t$):
\begin{itemize}
    \item Wave equation $u_{xx} - u_{yy} = 0$: $R = 1$, $S = 0$, $T = -1$, $S^2 - 4RT = 4 > 0$: hyperbolic.
    \item Heat equation $u_{xx} - u_y = 0$: $R = 1$, $S = 0$, $T = 0$, $S^2 - 4RT = 0$: parabolic. (The term $-u_y$ is of lower order; it is part of $f$.)
    \item Laplace's equation $u_{xx} + u_{yy} = 0$: $R = 1$, $S = 0$, $T = 1$, $S^2 - 4RT = -4 < 0$: elliptic.
\end{itemize}

\emph{Why the names?} The quadratic form $R\xi^2 + S\xi\eta + T\eta^2$ (replace $\partial_x \to \xi$, $\partial_y \to \eta$ in the principal part) is a hyperbola, parabola or ellipse when set equal to $1$, according to exactly the same sign condition, as in the classification of conics in analytic geometry.

\emph{The type may change from point to point} when $R, S, T$ depend on $(x,y)$. For example, for $y\,u_{xx} + u_{yy} = 0$ we get $S^2 - 4RT = -4y$: the equation is elliptic in $y > 0$, hyperbolic in $y < 0$ and parabolic on the line $y = 0$. So we always classify in a region where the sign of $S^2 - 4RT$ is constant.

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Change of independent variables}\label{ssec:changevar}

The idea is to introduce new independent variables
$$ \xi = \xi(x,y), \qquad \eta = \eta(x,y), $$
chosen so that in the new variables the principal part becomes as simple as possible. We assume $\xi, \eta \in C^2(\Omega)$ and that the \emph{Jacobian}
$$ J := \frac{\partial(\xi,\eta)}{\partial(x,y)} = \det\begin{pmatrix} \xi_x & \xi_y \\ \eta_x & \eta_y \end{pmatrix} = \xi_x\eta_y - \xi_y\eta_x $$
is nonzero in $\Omega$. Then, by the Inverse Function Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:inverse}), near each point the map $(x,y) \mapsto (\xi,\eta)$ is invertible with a $C^2$ inverse $(\xi,\eta) \mapsto (x,y)$. So $(\xi,\eta)$ are honest new coordinates, and we can express everything in them.

We define the new unknown $w$ by
$$ u(x,y) = w\big(\xi(x,y),\ \eta(x,y)\big), $$
i.e.\ $w(\xi,\eta)$ is the same function $u$ written in the new coordinates. ($w \in C^2$, since $u$ and the inverse map are $C^2$.) In examples one usually keeps the letter $u$ and writes $u_\xi$, $u_{\xi\eta}$, etc.; here we write $w$ to make clear which function is differentiated with respect to which variables.

\subsubsection{The first derivatives}
$w$ depends on $x$ through \emph{both} $\xi$ and $\eta$. By the chain rule,
\begin{align}
u_x &= w_\xi\,\xi_x + w_\eta\,\eta_x, \label{eq:ux}\\
u_y &= w_\xi\,\xi_y + w_\eta\,\eta_y. \label{eq:uy}
\end{align}
Here $w_\xi$, $w_\eta$ are evaluated at $(\xi(x,y),\eta(x,y))$, and $\xi_x$, $\eta_x$, \dots at $(x,y)$.

\subsubsection{The second derivatives}
\emph{Computing $u_{xx}$.} Differentiate \eqref{eq:ux} with respect to $x$. Each term is a product, so we use the product rule:
$$ u_{xx} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\big(w_\xi\big)\,\xi_x + w_\xi\,\xi_{xx} + \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\big(w_\eta\big)\,\eta_x + w_\eta\,\eta_{xx}. $$
Now $w_\xi$ and $w_\eta$ are again functions of $(\xi,\eta)$, so the chain rule \eqref{eq:ux} applies to them as well (with $w$ replaced by $w_\xi$, resp.\ $w_\eta$):
$$ \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\big(w_\xi\big) = w_{\xi\xi}\,\xi_x + w_{\xi\eta}\,\eta_x, \qquad \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\big(w_\eta\big) = w_{\eta\xi}\,\xi_x + w_{\eta\eta}\,\eta_x. $$
Substituting, and using $w_{\eta\xi} = w_{\xi\eta}$ ($w \in C^2$),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uxx}
u_{xx} = w_{\xi\xi}\,\xi_x^2 + 2w_{\xi\eta}\,\xi_x\eta_x + w_{\eta\eta}\,\eta_x^2 + w_\xi\,\xi_{xx} + w_\eta\,\eta_{xx}.
\end{equation}

\emph{Computing $u_{xy}$.} Differentiate \eqref{eq:ux} with respect to $y$, in the same way:
\begin{align*}
u_{xy} &= \big(w_{\xi\xi}\,\xi_y + w_{\xi\eta}\,\eta_y\big)\xi_x + w_\xi\,\xi_{xy} + \big(w_{\eta\xi}\,\xi_y + w_{\eta\eta}\,\eta_y\big)\eta_x + w_\eta\,\eta_{xy},
\end{align*}
that is,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uxy}
u_{xy} = w_{\xi\xi}\,\xi_x\xi_y + w_{\xi\eta}\,(\xi_x\eta_y + \xi_y\eta_x) + w_{\eta\eta}\,\eta_x\eta_y + w_\xi\,\xi_{xy} + w_\eta\,\eta_{xy}.
\end{equation}

\emph{Computing $u_{yy}$.} Differentiate \eqref{eq:uy} with respect to $y$; it is \eqref{eq:uxx} with $x$ replaced by $y$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uyy}
u_{yy} = w_{\xi\xi}\,\xi_y^2 + 2w_{\xi\eta}\,\xi_y\eta_y + w_{\eta\eta}\,\eta_y^2 + w_\xi\,\xi_{yy} + w_\eta\,\eta_{yy}.
\end{equation}

\emph{Observe the structure.} Each second derivative of $u$ consists of (i) second derivatives of $w$ multiplied by \emph{products of first derivatives} of $\xi, \eta$, and (ii) first derivatives of $w$ multiplied by \emph{second derivatives} of $\xi, \eta$. Only part (i) contributes to the new principal part.

\subsubsection{The transformed equation}
Substitute \eqref{eq:uxx}, \eqref{eq:uxy}, \eqref{eq:uyy} into \eqref{eq:pde2}, i.e.\ multiply them by $R$, $S$, $T$ respectively and add. Collecting the coefficients of $w_{\xi\xi}$, $w_{\xi\eta}$, $w_{\eta\eta}$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:newpde}
A\,w_{\xi\xi} + B\,w_{\xi\eta} + C\,w_{\eta\eta} + F(\xi,\eta,w,w_\xi,w_\eta) = 0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
A &= R\,\xi_x^2 + S\,\xi_x\xi_y + T\,\xi_y^2, \label{eq:A}\\
B &= 2R\,\xi_x\eta_x + S\,(\xi_x\eta_y + \xi_y\eta_x) + 2T\,\xi_y\eta_y, \label{eq:B}\\
C &= R\,\eta_x^2 + S\,\eta_x\eta_y + T\,\eta_y^2, \label{eq:C}
\end{align}
and $F$ collects all the rest:
$$ F = \big(R\xi_{xx} + S\xi_{xy} + T\xi_{yy}\big)w_\xi + \big(R\eta_{xx} + S\eta_{xy} + T\eta_{yy}\big)w_\eta + f, $$
with $f$ evaluated at $(x, y, w, u_x, u_y)$, $u_x$ and $u_y$ replaced by \eqref{eq:ux}, \eqref{eq:uy}, and finally all $x$, $y$ expressed through $\xi$, $\eta$ by the inverse map.

\emph{How to remember $A$, $B$, $C$.} $A$ is the principal symbol $R p^2 + Spq + Tq^2$ evaluated at $(p,q) = (\xi_x,\xi_y)$, i.e.\ at the gradient of $\xi$; $C$ is the same expression at the gradient of $\eta$; and $B$ is the corresponding ``mixed'' (polarized) expression. In matrix form, with
$$ M := \begin{pmatrix} R & S/2 \\ S/2 & T \end{pmatrix}, \qquad P := \begin{pmatrix} \xi_x & \xi_y \\ \eta_x & \eta_y \end{pmatrix}, \qquad M' := \begin{pmatrix} A & B/2 \\ B/2 & C \end{pmatrix}, $$
one checks directly (multiply out) that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:PMPt}
M' = P\,M\,P^{T}.
\end{equation}
For example, the $(1,1)$ entry of $PMP^T$ is $(\xi_x,\xi_y)M(\xi_x,\xi_y)^T = R\xi_x^2 + S\xi_x\xi_y + T\xi_y^2 = A$, and the $(1,2)$ entry is $(\xi_x,\xi_y)M(\eta_x,\eta_y)^T = R\xi_x\eta_x + \frac S2(\xi_x\eta_y + \xi_y\eta_x) + T\xi_y\eta_y = \frac B2$.

\begin{theorem}[The type is invariant]\label{thm:typeinv}
Under a change of variables with $J \neq 0$,
$$ B^2 - 4AC = (S^2 - 4RT)\,J^2. $$
In particular the new equation \eqref{eq:newpde} has the same type as \eqref{eq:pde2} at corresponding points.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take determinants in \eqref{eq:PMPt}: $\det M' = \det P\cdot\det M\cdot\det P^T = J^2\det M$. Now $\det M = RT - \frac{S^2}{4} = -\frac14(S^2 - 4RT)$ and likewise $\det M' = -\frac14(B^2 - 4AC)$. Multiply by $-4$. Since $J^2 > 0$, the two discriminants have the same sign.
\end{proof}

So the type is a property of the equation, not of the coordinates. What we \emph{can} change by choosing $\xi,\eta$ cleverly is the \emph{form} of the principal part. The simplest forms, called \emph{canonical forms}, are:
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Type} & \textbf{Canonical form} & \textbf{Model} \\ \hline
hyperbolic & $w_{\xi\eta} + \text{(lower order)} = 0$ & wave equation \\ \hline
parabolic & $w_{\eta\eta} + \text{(lower order)} = 0$ & heat equation \\ \hline
elliptic & $w_{\alpha\alpha} + w_{\beta\beta} + \text{(lower order)} = 0$ & Laplace's equation \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Characteristics: how to choose $\xi$ and $\eta$}

\subsubsection{Killing the coefficient $A$}
We try to choose $\xi$ so that $A = 0$, i.e.
$$ R\,\xi_x^2 + S\,\xi_x\xi_y + T\,\xi_y^2 = 0. $$
Suppose $\xi_y \neq 0$, and divide by $\xi_y^2$. Writing $\lambda := \dfrac{\xi_x}{\xi_y}$, the condition becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:charq}
R\lambda^2 + S\lambda + T = 0.
\end{equation}
This is the \emph{characteristic equation} of \eqref{eq:pde2}: a quadratic in $\lambda$ whose coefficients are those of the principal part. Its roots are
$$ \lambda_{1,2} = \frac{-S \pm \sqrt{S^2 - 4RT}}{2R} \qquad (R \neq 0), $$
so the discriminant decides everything:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{hyperbolic}: two distinct real roots $\lambda_1 \neq \lambda_2$;
    \item \textbf{parabolic}: one repeated real root $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = -\frac{S}{2R}$;
    \item \textbf{elliptic}: two complex conjugate roots $\lambda = m \pm in$, $n \neq 0$.
\end{itemize}
(If $R = 0$ at a point, we interchange the roles of $x$ and $y$, i.e.\ use $\mu = \xi_y/\xi_x$ and $T\mu^2 + S\mu + R = 0$. If $R = T = 0$, the equation is $S u_{xy} + \dots = 0$, already in hyperbolic canonical form.)

\subsubsection{From $\lambda$ to an ODE: the characteristic curves}
The condition $\xi_x = \lambda(x,y)\,\xi_y$ is a first-order PDE for $\xi$. We solve it by looking at the \emph{level curves} of $\xi$.

\emph{If $\xi$ satisfies $\xi_x = \lambda\xi_y$, its level curves solve an ODE.} Let $\xi(x,y) = c$ be a level curve, written as a graph $y = y(x)$. Differentiating the identity $\xi(x,y(x)) = c$ with respect to $x$ (chain rule):
$$ \xi_x + \xi_y\,\frac{dy}{dx} = 0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{\xi_x}{\xi_y} = -\lambda. $$
So the level curves of $\xi$ satisfy the \emph{characteristic ODE}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:charode}
\frac{dy}{dx} + \lambda(x,y) = 0.
\end{equation}

\emph{Conversely, a first integral of the ODE gives $\xi$.} Suppose we have integrated \eqref{eq:charode} and written its general solution in the form
$$ \varphi(x,y) = c \qquad (c \text{ an arbitrary constant}), $$
with $\varphi \in C^2$, $\varphi_y \neq 0$. Then $\varphi$ is constant along every solution curve $y(x)$: $\frac{d}{dx}\varphi(x,y(x)) = \varphi_x + \varphi_y y' = \varphi_x - \lambda\varphi_y = 0$. Through every point of $\Omega$ passes a solution curve (by the existence theorem for ODEs), so $\varphi_x = \lambda\varphi_y$ at every point. Therefore $\xi := \varphi$ satisfies $A = 0$.

The curves $\varphi(x,y) = c$ are called the \emph{characteristic curves} (or \emph{characteristics}) of \eqref{eq:pde2}. Recall the lines $x \pm ct = $ const for the wave equation in Section~\ref{sssec:dod}: they are exactly the characteristics of $u_{tt} - c^2u_{xx} = 0$.

\emph{Summary.} To make $A = 0$: solve $R\lambda^2 + S\lambda + T = 0$, solve the ODE $\frac{dy}{dx} + \lambda = 0$, write its solution as $\varphi(x,y) = c$, and take $\xi = \varphi$. The same applies to $\eta$ and $C$, since $C$ is the same expression with $\eta$ in place of $\xi$.

\subsubsection{The three cases}

\paragraph{Hyperbolic case ($S^2 - 4RT > 0$).} Two distinct real roots $\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$ give two families of real characteristics $\varphi_1 = c_1$, $\varphi_2 = c_2$. Set
$$ \xi = \varphi_1(x,y), \qquad \eta = \varphi_2(x,y). $$
Then $A = 0$ (since $\xi_x = \lambda_1\xi_y$) and $C = 0$ (since $\eta_x = \lambda_2\eta_y$). The Jacobian is
$$ J = \xi_x\eta_y - \xi_y\eta_x = \lambda_1\xi_y\eta_y - \xi_y\lambda_2\eta_y = (\lambda_1 - \lambda_2)\,\xi_y\eta_y \neq 0. $$
By Theorem~\ref{thm:typeinv}, $B^2 = B^2 - 4AC = (S^2 - 4RT)J^2 > 0$, so $B \neq 0$. Dividing \eqref{eq:newpde} by $B$:
$$ w_{\xi\eta} = -\frac{F}{B} \qquad (\text{first canonical form}). $$
If one prefers, put further $p = \xi + \eta$, $q = \xi - \eta$; since $w_\xi = W_p + W_q$ and $w_{\xi\eta} = W_{pp} - W_{qq}$ for $W(p,q) = w$, this gives $W_{pp} - W_{qq} = $ lower order (second canonical form, the form of the wave equation).

\paragraph{Parabolic case ($S^2 - 4RT = 0$).} There is only one root $\lambda = -\frac{S}{2R}$, hence one family of characteristics $\varphi = c$. Set
$$ \xi = \varphi(x,y), \qquad \eta = \text{any } C^2 \text{ function with } J \neq 0 \ (\text{often simply } \eta = x \text{ or } \eta = y). $$
Then $A = 0$. Now the important point: \emph{$B$ vanishes automatically.} Indeed, by Theorem~\ref{thm:typeinv},
$$ B^2 - 4AC = (S^2 - 4RT)J^2 = 0, \qquad \text{and } A = 0, \qquad \text{so } B^2 = 0. $$
This is why, in parabolic examples, all terms with $w_{\xi\xi}$ and $w_{\xi\eta}$ cancel. Finally $C \neq 0$: since $S^2 = 4RT$, one checks $C = R\eta_x^2 + S\eta_x\eta_y + T\eta_y^2 = R(\eta_x - \lambda\eta_y)^2$, while $J = \xi_x\eta_y - \xi_y\eta_x = \xi_y(\lambda\eta_y - \eta_x)$; so $J \neq 0$ forces $\eta_x - \lambda\eta_y \neq 0$ and $C \ne 0$. Dividing by $C$:
$$ w_{\eta\eta} = -\frac{F}{C} \qquad (\text{canonical form, the form of the heat equation}). $$
The second variable $\eta$ is not unique; the only requirement is $J \ne 0$, i.e.\ $\eta$ must not be constant along the characteristics.

\paragraph{Elliptic case ($S^2 - 4RT < 0$).} The roots $\lambda, \bar\lambda$ are complex, and so is the characteristic ODE: there are no real characteristics. Integrating \eqref{eq:charode} formally with $\lambda = m + in$ we get a complex-valued $\xi = \varphi(x,y) = \alpha(x,y) + i\beta(x,y)$ with $A(\xi) = 0$ (and $\eta = \bar\xi$, which adds nothing new). We use its real and imaginary parts as the new real variables:
$$ \alpha = \operatorname{Re}\xi = \frac{\xi + \bar\xi}{2}, \qquad \beta = \operatorname{Im}\xi = \frac{\xi - \bar\xi}{2i}. $$

\emph{Why this works.} Write $Q(\varphi,\psi) := R\varphi_x\psi_x + \frac S2(\varphi_x\psi_y + \varphi_y\psi_x) + T\varphi_y\psi_y$; this is symmetric and bilinear, and \eqref{eq:A}--\eqref{eq:C} say $A = Q(\xi,\xi)$, $B = 2Q(\xi,\eta)$, $C = Q(\eta,\eta)$. Expanding $Q(\alpha + i\beta, \alpha + i\beta) = 0$ by bilinearity:
$$ Q(\alpha,\alpha) - Q(\beta,\beta) + 2i\,Q(\alpha,\beta) = 0. $$
Real and imaginary parts give $Q(\alpha,\alpha) = Q(\beta,\beta)$ and $Q(\alpha,\beta) = 0$. So in the real variables $(\alpha,\beta)$ the new coefficients are
$$ A' = Q(\alpha,\alpha), \qquad B' = 2Q(\alpha,\beta) = 0, \qquad C' = Q(\beta,\beta) = A', $$
and $A' \ne 0$ (else the whole principal part would vanish, contradicting Theorem~\ref{thm:typeinv}). Dividing by $A'$:
$$ w_{\alpha\alpha} + w_{\beta\beta} = -\frac{F}{A'} \qquad (\text{canonical form, the form of Laplace's equation}). $$
(One also checks $J = \alpha_x\beta_y - \alpha_y\beta_x \neq 0$ in each example. In general, the existence of such $\xi$ requires more regularity of the coefficients; in our examples $\xi$ is found explicitly.)

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Step-by-step procedure to reduce to canonical form}

\paragraph{Step 1: Identify coefficients and classify.} Extract $R$, $S$ and $T$ from the PDE and evaluate $S^2 - 4RT$ to determine the type (and the region where it holds).

\paragraph{Step 2: Find the characteristic roots.} Form the characteristic quadratic equation
$$ R\lambda^2 + S\lambda + T = 0 $$
and solve for $\lambda$ to find the roots $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item hyperbolic: two distinct real roots;
    \item parabolic: one repeated real root ($\lambda_1 = \lambda_2$);
    \item elliptic: two complex conjugate roots ($\lambda = m \pm in$).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Step 3: Solve the characteristic ODEs.} Set up the ordinary differential equations
$$ \frac{dy}{dx} + \lambda_1 = 0 \qquad\text{and}\qquad \frac{dy}{dx} + \lambda_2 = 0. $$
Integrate both to find the characteristic curves $\varphi_1(x,y) = c_1$ and $\varphi_2(x,y) = c_2$. (Write the answer with the constant alone on one side.)

\paragraph{Step 4: Define the canonical variables $(\xi,\eta)$.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Hyperbolic: set $\xi = \varphi_1(x,y)$ and $\eta = \varphi_2(x,y)$.
    \item Parabolic: set $\xi = \varphi_1(x,y)$ and choose $\eta$ as any independent function of $x$ and $y$ (often simply $\eta = x$ or $\eta = y$), checking $J \ne 0$.
    \item Elliptic: the roots give a complex function $\xi = \varphi_1$. Take its real and imaginary parts $\alpha = \operatorname{Re}\xi$, $\beta = \operatorname{Im}\xi$ as the new variables.
\end{itemize}
In all cases check that the Jacobian $J = \xi_x\eta_y - \xi_y\eta_x$ is nonzero in the region considered.

\paragraph{Step 5: Apply the chain rule.} Calculate the required partial derivatives in terms of the new variables:
$$ u_x = u_\xi\,\xi_x + u_\eta\,\eta_x, \qquad u_y = u_\xi\,\xi_y + u_\eta\,\eta_y. $$
Differentiate again to find $u_{xx}$, $u_{xy}$ and $u_{yy}$, using \eqref{eq:uxx}--\eqref{eq:uyy} or (better, in examples) by differentiating $u_x$ and $u_y$ directly. Remember: when differentiating $u_\xi$ or $u_\eta$ with respect to $x$ or $y$, the chain rule must be applied again.

\paragraph{Step 6: Substitute and simplify.} Plug these new derivative expressions back into the original PDE. The equation will collapse into its simplified canonical form: in the hyperbolic case the $u_{\xi\xi}$ and $u_{\eta\eta}$ terms cancel; in the parabolic case the $u_{\xi\xi}$ and $u_{\xi\eta}$ terms cancel; in the elliptic case the mixed term cancels and the coefficients of $u_{\alpha\alpha}$, $u_{\beta\beta}$ become equal. Divide by the remaining coefficient and replace any remaining $x$ and $y$ by their expressions in terms of the new variables.

\emph{A check.} Before the long computation of Step 5 one can compute $A$, $B$, $C$ from \eqref{eq:A}--\eqref{eq:C}; they must come out as predicted ($A = C = 0$, resp.\ $A = B = 0$, resp.\ $B = 0$ and $A = C$). If not, there is an error in Steps 2--4.

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Worked examples}

In the examples we follow the usual practice and keep the letter $u$ for the unknown in the new variables, writing $u_\xi$, $u_{\xi\eta}$, etc.

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic equations}

\paragraph{Example 1.} $u_{xx} + 4u_{xy} + 3u_{yy} = 0$.

\emph{1. Classify.} $R = 1$, $S = 4$, $T = 3$. Discriminant $S^2 - 4RT = 16 - 12 = 4 > 0$: hyperbolic in all of $\mathbb{R}^2$.

\emph{2. Roots.} $\lambda^2 + 4\lambda + 3 = (\lambda + 1)(\lambda + 3) = 0$, so $\lambda_1 = -1$, $\lambda_2 = -3$.

\emph{3. ODEs.}
$$ \frac{dy}{dx} - 1 = 0 \implies y = x + c_1 \implies y - x = c_1; \qquad \frac{dy}{dx} - 3 = 0 \implies y - 3x = c_2. $$
The characteristics are two families of parallel straight lines, of slopes $1$ and $3$.

\emph{4. Variables.} $\xi = y - x$, $\eta = y - 3x$. Then $\xi_x = -1$, $\xi_y = 1$, $\eta_x = -3$, $\eta_y = 1$, and all second derivatives of $\xi,\eta$ vanish. $J = \xi_x\eta_y - \xi_y\eta_x = -1 + 3 = 2 \ne 0$.

\emph{5. Derivatives.}
$$ u_x = u_\xi\xi_x + u_\eta\eta_x = -u_\xi - 3u_\eta, \qquad u_y = u_\xi + u_\eta. $$
Differentiate again (each of $u_\xi$, $u_\eta$ is again differentiated by the chain rule; e.g.\ $\partial_x(u_\xi) = u_{\xi\xi}\xi_x + u_{\xi\eta}\eta_x = -u_{\xi\xi} - 3u_{\xi\eta}$):
\begin{align*}
u_{xx} &= \partial_x(-u_\xi - 3u_\eta) = -(-u_{\xi\xi} - 3u_{\xi\eta}) - 3(-u_{\xi\eta} - 3u_{\eta\eta}) = u_{\xi\xi} + 6u_{\xi\eta} + 9u_{\eta\eta}, \\
u_{xy} &= \partial_y(-u_\xi - 3u_\eta) = -(u_{\xi\xi} + u_{\xi\eta}) - 3(u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}) = -u_{\xi\xi} - 4u_{\xi\eta} - 3u_{\eta\eta}, \\
u_{yy} &= \partial_y(u_\xi + u_\eta) = (u_{\xi\xi} + u_{\xi\eta}) + (u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}) = u_{\xi\xi} + 2u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}.
\end{align*}

\emph{6. Substitute.} $u_{xx} + 4u_{xy} + 3u_{yy}$ has
$$ u_{\xi\xi}: \ 1 - 4 + 3 = 0, \qquad u_{\xi\eta}: \ 6 - 16 + 6 = -4, \qquad u_{\eta\eta}: \ 9 - 12 + 3 = 0. $$
So the equation becomes $-4u_{\xi\eta} = 0$, i.e.\ the canonical form
$$ u_{\xi\eta} = 0. $$
(Check: $B^2 - 4AC = 16 = (S^2 - 4RT)J^2 = 4\cdot 4$.)

\emph{Bonus: the general solution.} $u_{\xi\eta} = (u_\xi)_\eta = 0$ means $u_\xi$ does not depend on $\eta$: $u_\xi = \tilde F(\xi)$. Integrating in $\xi$: $u = F(\xi) + G(\eta)$, with $F' = \tilde F$ and $G$ arbitrary. Hence
$$ u(x,y) = F(y - x) + G(y - 3x) $$
for arbitrary $C^2$ functions $F, G$. This is exactly how the general solution $F(x + ct) + G(x - ct)$ of the wave equation arises: for $u_{tt} - c^2u_{xx} = 0$ the same procedure gives $\xi = x + ct$, $\eta = x - ct$ and $u_{\xi\eta} = 0$.

\paragraph{Example 2.} $u_{xx} - x^2u_{yy} = 0$ \quad ($x \ne 0$).

\emph{1. Classify.} $R = 1$, $S = 0$, $T = -x^2$. Discriminant $0 - 4(1)(-x^2) = 4x^2 > 0$ for $x \neq 0$: hyperbolic in $\{x \neq 0\}$ (parabolic on the line $x = 0$).

\emph{2. Roots.} $\lambda^2 - x^2 = 0$, so $\lambda_1 = x$, $\lambda_2 = -x$.

\emph{3. ODEs.}
$$ \frac{dy}{dx} + x = 0 \implies y = -\frac{x^2}{2} + c_1 \implies y + \frac{x^2}{2} = c_1; \qquad \frac{dy}{dx} - x = 0 \implies y - \frac{x^2}{2} = c_2. $$
The characteristics are two families of parabolas.

\emph{4. Variables.} $\xi = y + \frac{x^2}{2}$, $\eta = y - \frac{x^2}{2}$. Then
$$ \xi_x = x, \quad \xi_y = 1, \quad \eta_x = -x, \quad \eta_y = 1, \quad \xi_{xx} = 1, \quad \eta_{xx} = -1, $$
all other second derivatives being $0$. $J = \xi_x\eta_y - \xi_y\eta_x = x + x = 2x \neq 0$ for $x \neq 0$.

\emph{5. Derivatives.}
$$ u_x = x\,u_\xi - x\,u_\eta = x(u_\xi - u_\eta), \qquad u_y = u_\xi + u_\eta. $$
For $u_{xx}$ use the product rule on $x\cdot(u_\xi - u_\eta)$: the derivative of the factor $x$ is $1$, and
$$ \partial_x(u_\xi - u_\eta) = (u_{\xi\xi}\,x + u_{\xi\eta}(-x)) - (u_{\eta\xi}\,x + u_{\eta\eta}(-x)) = x(u_{\xi\xi} - 2u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}). $$
Hence
$$ u_{xx} = (u_\xi - u_\eta) + x^2(u_{\xi\xi} - 2u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}) = x^2u_{\xi\xi} - 2x^2u_{\xi\eta} + x^2u_{\eta\eta} + u_\xi - u_\eta, $$
$$ u_{yy} = \partial_y(u_\xi + u_\eta) = u_{\xi\xi} + 2u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}. $$

\emph{6. Substitute.}
$$ \big(x^2u_{\xi\xi} - 2x^2u_{\xi\eta} + x^2u_{\eta\eta} + u_\xi - u_\eta\big) - x^2\big(u_{\xi\xi} + 2u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}\big) = 0 $$
simplifies to $-4x^2u_{\xi\eta} + u_\xi - u_\eta = 0$. Since $\xi - \eta = x^2$, the canonical form is
$$ u_{\xi\eta} = \frac{1}{4(\xi - \eta)}\,(u_\xi - u_\eta) \qquad (\xi > \eta). $$
Note that $F$ is not zero here: the second derivatives $\xi_{xx}$, $\eta_{xx}$ produce first-order terms.

\subsubsection{Parabolic equations}

\paragraph{Example 3.} $4u_{xx} + 4u_{xy} + u_{yy} + u_x = 0$.

\emph{1. Classify.} $R = 4$, $S = 4$, $T = 1$. Discriminant $16 - 16 = 0$: parabolic in $\mathbb{R}^2$.

\emph{2. Roots.} $4\lambda^2 + 4\lambda + 1 = (2\lambda + 1)^2 = 0$, so $\lambda = -\frac12$ (double root).

\emph{3. ODE.} $\dfrac{dy}{dx} - \dfrac12 = 0 \implies y - \dfrac x2 = c \implies 2y - x = c_1$ (any function of a constant is a constant, so we may multiply by $2$).

\emph{4. Variables.} $\xi = 2y - x$ and, as second variable, $\eta = y$. Then $\xi_x = -1$, $\xi_y = 2$, $\eta_x = 0$, $\eta_y = 1$; second derivatives vanish. $J = (-1)(1) - (2)(0) = -1 \neq 0$. (The choice $\eta = x$ would also do: $J = -2$.)

\emph{5. Derivatives.}
$$ u_x = -u_\xi, \qquad u_y = 2u_\xi + u_\eta, $$
\begin{align*}
u_{xx} &= \partial_x(-u_\xi) = -(u_{\xi\xi}(-1) + u_{\xi\eta}\cdot 0) = u_{\xi\xi}, \\
u_{xy} &= \partial_y(-u_\xi) = -(2u_{\xi\xi} + u_{\xi\eta}), \\
u_{yy} &= \partial_y(2u_\xi + u_\eta) = 2(2u_{\xi\xi} + u_{\xi\eta}) + (2u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}) = 4u_{\xi\xi} + 4u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}.
\end{align*}

\emph{6. Substitute.} $4u_{xx} + 4u_{xy} + u_{yy}$ has
$$ u_{\xi\xi}: \ 4 - 8 + 4 = 0, \qquad u_{\xi\eta}: \ -4 + 4 = 0, \qquad u_{\eta\eta}: \ 1, $$
as predicted (both $A$ and $B$ vanish). Adding $u_x = -u_\xi$, the equation becomes $u_{\eta\eta} - u_\xi = 0$, i.e.
$$ u_\xi = u_{\eta\eta}. $$
This is the heat equation, with $\xi$ playing the role of time and $\eta$ of space.

\paragraph{Example 4.} $y^2u_{xx} - 2xy\,u_{xy} + x^2u_{yy} = 0$ \quad ($y \ne 0$).

\emph{1. Classify.} $R = y^2$, $S = -2xy$, $T = x^2$. Discriminant $(-2xy)^2 - 4y^2x^2 = 0$: parabolic.

\emph{2. Roots.} $y^2\lambda^2 - 2xy\lambda + x^2 = (y\lambda - x)^2 = 0$, so $\lambda = \dfrac{x}{y}$ (double root).

\emph{3. ODE.} $\dfrac{dy}{dx} + \dfrac xy = 0 \implies y\,dy + x\,dx = 0 \implies \dfrac{y^2}{2} + \dfrac{x^2}{2} = c \implies x^2 + y^2 = c_1$. The characteristics are circles centred at the origin.

\emph{4. Variables.} $\xi = x^2 + y^2$, and $\eta = x$. Then
$$ \xi_x = 2x, \quad \xi_y = 2y, \quad \xi_{xx} = \xi_{yy} = 2, \quad \xi_{xy} = 0, \qquad \eta_x = 1, \quad \eta_y = 0, $$
and $J = (2x)(0) - (2y)(1) = -2y \neq 0$ for $y \neq 0$.

\emph{5. Derivatives.}
$$ u_x = 2x\,u_\xi + u_\eta, \qquad u_y = 2y\,u_\xi. $$
Using $\partial_x(u_\xi) = 2x\,u_{\xi\xi} + u_{\xi\eta}$, $\partial_x(u_\eta) = 2x\,u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}$, $\partial_y(u_\xi) = 2y\,u_{\xi\xi}$, $\partial_y(u_\eta) = 2y\,u_{\xi\eta}$ and the product rule:
\begin{align*}
u_{xx} &= 2u_\xi + 2x(2x\,u_{\xi\xi} + u_{\xi\eta}) + (2x\,u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta}) = 4x^2u_{\xi\xi} + 4x\,u_{\xi\eta} + u_{\eta\eta} + 2u_\xi, \\
u_{xy} &= 2x(2y\,u_{\xi\xi}) + 2y\,u_{\xi\eta} = 4xy\,u_{\xi\xi} + 2y\,u_{\xi\eta}, \\
u_{yy} &= 2u_\xi + 2y(2y\,u_{\xi\xi}) = 4y^2u_{\xi\xi} + 2u_\xi.
\end{align*}

\emph{6. Substitute.}
\begin{align*}
y^2u_{xx} &= 4x^2y^2u_{\xi\xi} + 4xy^2u_{\xi\eta} + y^2u_{\eta\eta} + 2y^2u_\xi, \\
-2xy\,u_{xy} &= -8x^2y^2u_{\xi\xi} - 4xy^2u_{\xi\eta}, \\
x^2u_{yy} &= 4x^2y^2u_{\xi\xi} + 2x^2u_\xi.
\end{align*}
Adding: the $u_{\xi\xi}$ terms give $4 - 8 + 4 = 0$ and the $u_{\xi\eta}$ terms give $4 - 4 = 0$, leaving
$$ y^2u_{\eta\eta} + 2(x^2 + y^2)u_\xi = 0. $$
Now $x^2 + y^2 = \xi$ and $y^2 = \xi - x^2 = \xi - \eta^2$. Hence the canonical form is
$$ u_{\eta\eta} = -\frac{2\xi}{\xi - \eta^2}\,u_\xi \qquad (\xi > \eta^2). $$

\subsubsection{Elliptic equations}

\paragraph{Example 5.} $u_{xx} + 2u_{xy} + 2u_{yy} = 0$.

\emph{1. Classify.} $R = 1$, $S = 2$, $T = 2$. Discriminant $4 - 8 = -4 < 0$: elliptic in $\mathbb{R}^2$.

\emph{2. Roots.} $\lambda^2 + 2\lambda + 2 = 0 \implies \lambda = \dfrac{-2 \pm \sqrt{-4}}{2} = -1 \pm i$.

\emph{3. ODE.} Take $\lambda_1 = -1 + i$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx} + (-1 + i) = 0 \implies \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 1 - i \implies y = (1 - i)x + c \implies (y - x) + ix = c_1$.

\emph{4. Variables.} $\xi = (y - x) + ix$; real and imaginary parts
$$ \alpha = y - x, \qquad \beta = x. $$
Then $\alpha_x = -1$, $\alpha_y = 1$, $\beta_x = 1$, $\beta_y = 0$; second derivatives vanish. $J = \alpha_x\beta_y - \alpha_y\beta_x = 0 - 1 = -1 \neq 0$.

\emph{5. Derivatives.}
$$ u_x = -u_\alpha + u_\beta, \qquad u_y = u_\alpha, $$
\begin{align*}
u_{xx} &= \partial_x(-u_\alpha + u_\beta) = -(-u_{\alpha\alpha} + u_{\alpha\beta}) + (-u_{\alpha\beta} + u_{\beta\beta}) = u_{\alpha\alpha} - 2u_{\alpha\beta} + u_{\beta\beta}, \\
u_{xy} &= \partial_y(-u_\alpha + u_\beta) = -u_{\alpha\alpha} + u_{\alpha\beta}, \\
u_{yy} &= \partial_y(u_\alpha) = u_{\alpha\alpha}.
\end{align*}

\emph{6. Substitute.} $u_{xx} + 2u_{xy} + 2u_{yy}$ has
$$ u_{\alpha\alpha}: \ 1 - 2 + 2 = 1, \qquad u_{\alpha\beta}: \ -2 + 2 = 0, \qquad u_{\beta\beta}: \ 1. $$
The canonical form is Laplace's equation
$$ u_{\alpha\alpha} + u_{\beta\beta} = 0. $$
(Check: $B'^2 - 4A'C' = -4 = (S^2 - 4RT)J^2 = -4\cdot 1$.)

\paragraph{Example 6.} $u_{xx} + x^2u_{yy} = 0$ \quad ($x \neq 0$).

\emph{1. Classify.} $R = 1$, $S = 0$, $T = x^2$. Discriminant $0 - 4x^2 < 0$ for $x \neq 0$: elliptic in $\{x \neq 0\}$.

\emph{2. Roots.} $\lambda^2 + x^2 = 0$, so $\lambda = \pm ix$.

\emph{3. ODE.} Take $\lambda = -ix$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx} - ix = 0 \implies y = \dfrac{ix^2}{2} + c \implies$ multiplying by $i$: $iy + \dfrac{x^2}{2} = c_1$.

\emph{4. Variables.} $\xi = \dfrac{x^2}{2} + iy$; real and imaginary parts
$$ \alpha = \frac{x^2}{2}, \qquad \beta = y. $$
Then $\alpha_x = x$, $\alpha_{xx} = 1$, $\alpha_y = 0$, $\beta_x = 0$, $\beta_y = 1$. $J = \alpha_x\beta_y - \alpha_y\beta_x = x \neq 0$ for $x \neq 0$.

\emph{5. Derivatives.} $u_x = x\,u_\alpha$, $u_y = u_\beta$, and by the product rule
$$ u_{xx} = u_\alpha + x\,\partial_x(u_\alpha) = u_\alpha + x(x\,u_{\alpha\alpha}) = x^2u_{\alpha\alpha} + u_\alpha, \qquad u_{yy} = u_{\beta\beta}. $$

\emph{6. Substitute.}
$$ (x^2u_{\alpha\alpha} + u_\alpha) + x^2u_{\beta\beta} = 0 \implies x^2(u_{\alpha\alpha} + u_{\beta\beta}) + u_\alpha = 0. $$
Since $x^2 = 2\alpha$, the canonical form is
$$ u_{\alpha\alpha} + u_{\beta\beta} = -\frac{1}{2\alpha}\,u_\alpha \qquad (\alpha > 0). $$
As predicted, the mixed term is absent and $u_{\alpha\alpha}$, $u_{\beta\beta}$ have equal coefficients ($x^2$) before dividing.
